<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the modern <a href="/wiki/Island_nation" class="mw-redirect" title="Island nation">island nation</a>. For the <a href="/wiki/Island" title="Island">island</a> itself, see <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Madagascar" title="Geography of Madagascar">Geography of Madagascar</a>. For the film franchise, see <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_(franchise)" title="Madagascar (franchise)"><i>Madagascar</i> (franchise)</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Madagascar (disambiguation)">Madagascar (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

<p><b>Madagascar</b>, officially the <b>Republic of Madagascar</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-name_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-name-13">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> is an  <a href="/wiki/Island_country" title="Island country">island country</a> lying off the southeastern coast of <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a>. It is the world's <a href="/wiki/List_of_islands_by_area" title="List of islands by area">fourth largest island</a>, the <a href="/wiki/List_of_island_countries" title="List of island countries">second-largest island country</a> and the 46th largest country in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-world-atlas_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-world-atlas-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Its capital and largest city is <a href="/wiki/Antananarivo" title="Antananarivo">Antananarivo</a>.
</p><p>Madagascar consists of an <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Madagascar" title="Geography of Madagascar">eponymous main island</a>  and numerous smaller peripheral islands. Following the prehistoric breakup of the <a href="/wiki/Supercontinent" title="Supercontinent">supercontinent</a> <a href="/wiki/Gondwana" title="Gondwana">Gondwana</a>, Madagascar split from Africa during the Early Jurassic, around 180 million years ago, and split from the <a href="/wiki/Indian_subcontinent" title="Indian subcontinent">Indian subcontinent</a> around 90&#160;million years ago,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> allowing native plants and animals to evolve in relative isolation; consequently, it is a <a href="/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot" title="Biodiversity hotspot">biodiversity hotspot</a> and one of the world's 17 <a href="/wiki/Megadiverse_countries" title="Megadiverse countries">megadiverse countries</a>, with over 90% of <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Madagascar" title="Wildlife of Madagascar">wildlife</a> being endemic. The island has a subtropical to tropical maritime climate.
</p><p>Madagascar was first settled during or before the mid first millennium AD by <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_peoples" title="Austronesian peoples">Austronesian peoples</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-:4_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> presumably arriving on <a href="/wiki/Outrigger_boat" title="Outrigger boat">outrigger canoes</a> from present-day <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> These were joined around the ninth century AD by <a href="/wiki/Bantu_peoples" title="Bantu peoples">Bantu</a> migrants crossing the Mozambique Channel from East Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Other groups continued to settle on Madagascar over time, each one making lasting contributions to Malagasy cultural life. Subsequently, the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy people">Malagasy ethnic group</a> is often divided into 18 or more <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic groups of Madagascar">subgroups</a>, of which the largest are the <a href="/wiki/Merina_people" title="Merina people">Merina</a> of the central highlands.
</p><p>Until the late 18th century, the island of Madagascar was ruled by a fragmented assortment of shifting sociopolitical alliances. Beginning in the early 19th century, most of it was united and ruled as the <a href="/wiki/Merina_Kingdom" title="Merina Kingdom">Kingdom of Madagascar</a> by a <a href="/wiki/List_of_Imerina_monarchs" title="List of Imerina monarchs">series</a> of Merina <a href="/wiki/Andriana" title="Andriana">nobles</a>. The monarchy was ended in 1897 by the <a href="/wiki/French_colonial_empire" title="French colonial empire">annexation by France</a>, from which Madagascar gained independence in 1960. The country has since undergone four major constitutional periods, termed republics, and has been governed as a constitutional democracy since 1992. Following a <a href="/wiki/2009_Malagasy_political_crisis" title="2009 Malagasy political crisis">political crisis and military coup in 2009</a>, Madagascar underwent a <a href="/wiki/High_Transitional_Authority" title="High Transitional Authority">protracted transition towards</a> its fourth and current republic, with constitutional governance being restored in January 2014.
</p><p>Madagascar is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> (UN), the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> (AU), the <a href="/wiki/Southern_African_Development_Community" title="Southern African Development Community">Southern African Development Community</a> (SADC), and the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_Internationale_de_la_Francophonie" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation Internationale de la Francophonie">Organization Internationale de la Francophonie</a>. <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> are both <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official languages</a> of the state. <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> is the country's predominant religion, with a significant minority still practicing <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_mythology" title="Malagasy mythology">traditional faiths</a>. Madagascar is classified as a <a href="/wiki/Least_developed_countries" title="Least developed countries">least developed country</a> by the UN.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ecotourism" title="Ecotourism">Ecotourism</a> and agriculture, paired with greater investments in education, health and private enterprise, are key elements of its development strategy. Despite substantial economic growth since the early 2000s, income disparities have widened, and quality of life remains low for the majority of the population. Madagascar is experiencing <a href="/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_Madagascar_famine" title="2021–2022 Madagascar famine">an ongoing famine</a>, which experts argue is the first to be caused entirely by <a href="/wiki/Climate_change" title="Climate change">climate change</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy language</a>, the island of Madagascar is called <i>Madagasikara</i> (<small>Malagasy pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="mg-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Malagasy" title="Help:IPA/Malagasy">[madaɡasʲˈkʲarə̥]</a></span>) and its people are referred to as <i><a href="/wiki/Malagasy_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy people">Malagasy</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-NatGeo1_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NatGeo1-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MalagasyOrNot_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MalagasyOrNot-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The origin of the name is uncertain,<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> and is likely foreign, having been propagated in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> by Europeans.<sup id="cite_ref-Cousins2_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cousins2-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> If this is the case, it is unknown when the name was adopted by the inhabitants of the island. No single Malagasy-language name predating <i>Madagasikara</i> appears to have been used by the local population to refer to the island, although some communities had their own name for part or all of the land they inhabited.<sup id="cite_ref-Room_2006,_p._2302_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Room_2006,_p._2302-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One hypothesis relates <i>Madagascar</i> to the word <i><a href="/wiki/Malays_(ethnic_group)" title="Malays (ethnic group)">Malay</a></i>, referring to the <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_peoples" title="Austronesian peoples">Austronesian</a> origin of the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy people">Malagasy people</a> in modern-day <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>. In a map by <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_al-Idrisi" title="Muhammad al-Idrisi">Muhammad al-Idrisi</a> dating from the year 1154, the island is named <i>Gesira Malai</i>, or "Malay island" in <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a>. The inversion of this name to <i>Malai Gesira</i>, as it was known by the <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greeks</a>, is thought to be the precursor of the modern name of the island.<sup id="cite_ref-:022_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:022-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The name "Malay island" was later rendered in Latin as <i>Malichu</i>, an abbreviated form of <i>Malai Insula</i>, in the medieval <a href="/wiki/Hereford_Mappa_Mundi" title="Hereford Mappa Mundi">Hereford Mappa Mundi</a> as the name of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:022_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:022-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another hypothesis is that <i>Madagascar</i> is a corrupted transliteration of <a href="/wiki/Mogadishu" title="Mogadishu">Mogadishu</a>, the capital of <a href="/wiki/Somalia" title="Somalia">Somalia</a> and an important medieval Indian Ocean port. This would have resulted from 13th-century Venetian explorer <a href="/wiki/Marco_Polo" title="Marco Polo">Marco Polo</a> confusing the two locations in his memoirs, in which he mentions the land of <i>Madageiscar</i> to the south of <a href="/wiki/Socotra" title="Socotra">Socotra</a>. This name would then have been popularized on <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a> maps by Europeans.<sup id="cite_ref-Cousins2_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cousins2-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Room_2006,_p._2302_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Room_2006,_p._2302-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The name <i>Malagasikara</i>, or <b>Malagascar</b>, is also historically attested. A British state paper in 1699 records the arrival of eighty to ninety passengers from "Malagaskar" to <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> An 1882 edition of the British newspaper <a href="/wiki/The_Graphic" title="The Graphic">The Graphic</a> referred to "Malagascar" as the name of the island, stating that it is etymologically a word of <a href="/wiki/Malay_language" title="Malay language">Malay</a> origin, and may be related to the name of <a href="/wiki/Malacca_City" title="Malacca City">Malacca</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In 1891, Saleh bin Osman, a <a href="/wiki/Zanzibar" title="Zanzibar">Zanzibari</a> traveler, refers to the island as "Malagaskar" when recounting his journeys, including as part of the <a href="/wiki/Emin_Pasha_Relief_Expedition" title="Emin Pasha Relief Expedition">Emin Pasha Relief Expedition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> In 1905, Charles Basset wrote in his doctoral thesis that <i>Malagasikara</i> was the way the island is referred to by its natives, who emphasized that they were <i>Malagasy</i>, and not <i>Madagasy</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Madagascar" title="History of Madagascar">History of Madagascar</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_period">Early period</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early period">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg/220px-Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg/330px-Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg/440px-Malagasy_girls_Madagascar_Merina.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4288" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Malagasy_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy people">Malagasy</a> ancestry reflects a blend of Southeast Asian, Oceanian and Bantu (Southeast African) roots.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Traditionally, archaeologists have estimated that the earliest settlers arrived in successive waves in <a href="/wiki/Outrigger_canoe" class="mw-redirect" title="Outrigger canoe">outrigger canoes</a> from <a href="/wiki/South_Kalimantan" title="South Kalimantan">South Borneo</a>, possibly throughout the period between 350&#160;BCE and 550&#160;CE, while others are cautious about dates earlier than 250&#160;CE. In either case, these dates make Madagascar one of the latest major landmasses on Earth to be settled by humans, predating the settlement of <a href="/wiki/Iceland" title="Iceland">Iceland</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> It is proposed that <a href="/wiki/Ma%27anyan_people" title="Ma&#39;anyan people">Ma'anyan people</a> were brought as laborers and slaves by <a href="/wiki/Javanese_people" title="Javanese people">Javanese</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sumatra" title="Sumatra">Sumatran</a>-<a href="/wiki/Malay_(ethnic_group)" class="mw-redirect" title="Malay (ethnic group)">Malays</a> in their trading fleets to Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-Dewar19932_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dewar19932-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Burney20042_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burney20042-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Dates earlier than the mid-first millennium AD are not strongly supported.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon arrival, early settlers practiced <a href="/wiki/Slash-and-burn" title="Slash-and-burn">slash-and-burn</a> agriculture to clear the coastal rainforests for cultivation. The first settlers encountered Madagascar's abundance of <a href="/wiki/Pleistocene_megafauna" title="Pleistocene megafauna">megafauna</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> including 17 species of <a href="/wiki/Subfossil_lemur" title="Subfossil lemur">giant lemurs</a>, the large flightless <a href="/wiki/Elephant_bird" title="Elephant bird">elephant birds</a> (including possibly the largest bird to ever exist, <i><a href="/wiki/Aepyornis_maximus" class="mw-redirect" title="Aepyornis maximus">Aepyornis maximus</a></i>), the <a href="/wiki/Cryptoprocta_spelea" title="Cryptoprocta spelea">giant fossa</a>, and several species of <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_hippopotamus" title="Malagasy hippopotamus">Malagasy hippopotamus</a>, which have since become extinct because of hunting and habitat destruction.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> By 600&#160;CE, groups of these early settlers had begun clearing the forests of the central highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-Camp93_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Camp93-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arab traders first reached the island between the 7th and 9th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-Wink_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wink-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> A wave of <a href="/wiki/Bantu_languages" title="Bantu languages">Bantu</a>-speaking migrants from southeastern Africa arrived around 1000&#160;CE.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> South Indian Tamil merchants arrived around 11th century. They introduced the <a href="/wiki/Zebu" title="Zebu">zebu</a>, a type of long-horned humped cattle, which they kept in large herds.<sup id="cite_ref-Gade_1996_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gade_1996-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Irrigated paddy fields were developed in the central highland <a href="/wiki/Betsileo" class="mw-redirect" title="Betsileo">Betsileo</a> Kingdom and were extended with terraced paddies throughout the neighboring Kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Imerina" class="mw-redirect" title="Imerina">Imerina</a> a century later.<sup id="cite_ref-Camp93_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Camp93-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The rising intensity of land cultivation and the ever-increasing demand for zebu pasturage had largely transformed the central highlands from a forest ecosystem to grassland by the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Gade_1996_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gade_1996-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The oral histories of the Merina people, who may have arrived in the central highlands between 600 and 1,000 years ago, describe encountering an established population they called the <a href="/wiki/Vazimba" title="Vazimba">Vazimba</a>. Probably the descendants of an earlier and less technologically advanced Austronesian settlement wave, the Vazimba were assimilated or expelled from the highlands by the Merina kings <a href="/wiki/Andriamanelo" title="Andriamanelo">Andriamanelo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ralambo" title="Ralambo">Ralambo</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Andrianjaka" title="Andrianjaka">Andrianjaka</a> in the 16th and early 17th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-vazimbadjp_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vazimbadjp-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Today, the spirits of the Vazimba are revered as <i>tompontany</i> (ancestral masters of the land) by many traditional Malagasy communities.<sup id="cite_ref-Mythe_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mythe-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arab_and_European_contacts">Arab and European contacts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Arab and European contacts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_(Cabral_Armada).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg/220px-Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="222" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg/330px-Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg/440px-Detail_of_Diogo_Dias%27s_ship_%28Cabral_Armada%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="587" data-file-height="592" /></a><figcaption>European contact began in 1500 when <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_discoveries" class="mw-redirect" title="Portuguese discoveries">Portuguese explorer</a> <a href="/wiki/Diogo_Dias" title="Diogo Dias">Diogo Dias</a> recorded the island while participating in the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Portuguese_India_Armada_(Cabral,_1500)" title="2nd Portuguese India Armada (Cabral, 1500)">2nd Portuguese India Armadas</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Recorded_history" title="Recorded history">written history</a> of Madagascar began with the Arabs, who established trading posts along the northwest coast by at least the 10th century and introduced <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Arabic_script" title="Arabic script">Arabic script</a> (used to transcribe the Malagasy language in a form of writing known as <i><a href="/wiki/Sorabe" class="mw-redirect" title="Sorabe">sorabe</a></i>), Arab astrology, and other cultural elements.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>European contact began in 1500, when the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese</a> sea captain <a href="/wiki/Diogo_Dias" title="Diogo Dias">Diogo Dias</a> sighted the island, while participating in the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Portuguese_India_Armada_(Cabral,_1500)" title="2nd Portuguese India Armada (Cabral, 1500)">2nd Armada</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_India_Armadas" title="Portuguese India Armadas">Portuguese India Armadas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Sainte_Luce_Reserve" title="Sainte Luce Reserve">Matatana</a> was the first Portuguese settlement on the south coast, 10&#160;km west of <a href="/wiki/Fort_Dauphin_(Madagascar)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fort Dauphin (Madagascar)">Fort Dauphin</a>. In 1508, settlers there built a tower, a small village, and a stone column. This settlement was established in 1513 at the behest of the viceroy of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_India" title="Portuguese India">Portuguese India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jer%C3%B3nimo_de_Azevedo" title="Jerónimo de Azevedo">Jeronimo de Azevedo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Matatana.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Matatana.jpg/220px-Matatana.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Matatana.jpg/330px-Matatana.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Matatana.jpg/440px-Matatana.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3227" data-file-height="2109" /></a><figcaption>Matatana, represented in a picture of 1613, regarding a settlement of the beginning of the 16th century, in the Book of Humberto Leitão"</figcaption></figure>
<p>Contacts continued from the 1550s. Several colonization and conversion missions were ordered by King <a href="/wiki/John_III_of_Portugal" title="John III of Portugal">João III</a> and by the Viceroy of India, including one in 1553 by Baltazar Lobo de Sousa. In that mission, according to detailed descriptions by chroniclers Diogo do Couto and João de Barros, emissaries reached the inland via rivers and bays, exchanging goods and even converting one of the local kings.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The French established trading posts along the east coast in the late 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> From about 1774 to 1824, Madagascar gained prominence among pirates and European traders, particularly those involved in the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">trans-Atlantic slave trade</a>. The small island of <a href="/wiki/%C3%8Ele_Sainte-Marie" class="mw-redirect" title="Île Sainte-Marie">Nosy Boroha</a> off the northeastern coast of Madagascar has been proposed by some historians as the site of the legendary pirate utopia of <a href="/wiki/Libertalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Libertalia">Libertalia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Many European sailors were shipwrecked on the coasts of the island, among them <a href="/wiki/Robert_Drury_(sailor)" title="Robert Drury (sailor)">Robert Drury</a>, whose journal is one of the few written depictions of life in southern Madagascar during the 18th century.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The wealth generated by maritime trade spurred the rise of organized kingdoms on the island, some of which had grown quite powerful by the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Among these were the <a href="/wiki/Betsimisaraka_people" title="Betsimisaraka people">Betsimisaraka</a> alliance of the eastern coast and the <a href="/wiki/Sakalava" class="mw-redirect" title="Sakalava">Sakalava</a> chiefdoms of <a href="/wiki/Menabe" title="Menabe">Menabe</a> and <a href="/wiki/Boina_Kingdom" title="Boina Kingdom">Boina</a> on the west coast. The Kingdom of Imerina, located in the central highlands with its capital at the <a href="/wiki/Rova_of_Antananarivo" title="Rova of Antananarivo">royal palace</a> of Antananarivo, emerged at around the same time under the leadership of King Andriamanelo.<sup id="cite_ref-Ogot_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ogot-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Kingdom_of_Madagascar">Kingdom of Madagascar</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Kingdom of Madagascar">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Merina_Kingdom" title="Merina Kingdom">Merina Kingdom</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrianampoinimerina.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Andrianampoinimerina.jpg/180px-Andrianampoinimerina.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="224" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Andrianampoinimerina.jpg/270px-Andrianampoinimerina.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Andrianampoinimerina.jpg/360px-Andrianampoinimerina.jpg 2x" data-file-width="398" data-file-height="495" /></a><figcaption>King <a href="/wiki/Andrianampoinimerina" title="Andrianampoinimerina">Andrianampoinimerina</a> (1787–1810)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon its emergence in the early 17th century, the highland kingdom of Imerina was initially a minor power relative to the larger coastal kingdoms<sup id="cite_ref-Ogot_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ogot-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> and grew even weaker in the early 18th century when King <a href="/wiki/Andriamasinavalona" title="Andriamasinavalona">Andriamasinavalona</a> divided it among his four sons. Following almost a century of warring and famine, Imerina was reunited in 1793 by King <a href="/wiki/Andrianampoinimerina" title="Andrianampoinimerina">Andrianampoinimerina</a> (1787–1810).<sup id="cite_ref-Hodder_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hodder-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> From his initial capital <a href="/wiki/Ambohimanga" title="Ambohimanga">Ambohimanga</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> and later from the <a href="/wiki/Rova_of_Antananarivo" title="Rova of Antananarivo">Rova of Antananarivo</a>, this Merina king rapidly expanded his rule over neighboring principalities. His ambition to bring the entire island under his control was largely achieved by his son and successor, King <a href="/wiki/Radama_I" title="Radama I">Radama I</a> (1810–28), who was recognized by the British government as King of Madagascar. Radama concluded a treaty in 1817 with the British governor of <a href="/wiki/Mauritius" title="Mauritius">Mauritius</a> to abolish the lucrative slave trade in return for British military and financial assistance. Artisan missionary envoys from the <a href="/wiki/London_Missionary_Society" title="London Missionary Society">London Missionary Society</a> began arriving in 1818 and included such key figures as <a href="/wiki/James_Cameron_(missionary)" title="James Cameron (missionary)">James Cameron</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Jones_(missionary)" title="David Jones (missionary)">David Jones</a> and <a href="/wiki/David_Griffiths_(missionary)" title="David Griffiths (missionary)">David Griffiths</a>, who established schools, transcribed the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy language</a> using the <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Roman alphabet</a>, translated the Bible, and introduced a variety of new technologies to the island.<sup id="cite_ref-Ade_Ajayi_1998,_pp._413–422_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ade_Ajayi_1998,_pp._413–422-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Radama's successor, Queen <a href="/wiki/Ranavalona_I" title="Ranavalona I">Ranavalona I</a> (1828–61), responded to increasing political and cultural encroachment on the part of Britain and France by issuing a royal edict prohibiting the practice of <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Madagascar" title="Christianity in Madagascar">Christianity in Madagascar</a> and pressuring most foreigners to leave the territory. <a href="/wiki/William_Ellis_(missionary)" title="William Ellis (missionary)">William Ellis</a> of the <a href="/wiki/London_Missionary_Society" title="London Missionary Society">London Missionary Society</a> described his visits made during her reign in his book <i>Three Visits to Madagascar during the years 1853, 1854, and 1856</i>. The Queen made heavy use of the traditional practice of <i><a href="/wiki/Corv%C3%A9e" title="Corvée">fanompoana</a></i> (forced labor as tax payment) to complete public works projects and develop a standing army of between 20,000 and 30,000 <a href="/wiki/Merina" class="mw-redirect" title="Merina">Merina</a> soldiers, whom she deployed to pacify outlying regions of the island and further expand the Kingdom of Merina to encompass most of Madagascar. Residents of Madagascar could accuse one another of various crimes, including theft, Christianity and especially witchcraft, for which the ordeal of <i><a href="/wiki/Tangena" title="Tangena">tangena</a></i> was routinely obligatory. Between 1828 and 1861, the <i>tangena</i> ordeal caused about 3,000 deaths annually. In 1838, it was estimated that as many as 100,000 people in Imerina died as a result of the tangena ordeal, constituting roughly 20 percent of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-Stats_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stats-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The combination of regular warfare, disease, difficult forced labor, and harsh measures of justice resulted in a high mortality rate among soldiers and civilians alike during her 33-year reign; the population of Madagascar is estimated to have declined from around 5&#160;million to 2.5&#160;million between 1833 and 1839.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among those who continued to reside in Imerina were <a href="/wiki/Jean_Laborde" title="Jean Laborde">Jean Laborde</a>, an entrepreneur who developed munitions and other industries on behalf of the monarchy, and <a href="/wiki/Joseph-Fran%C3%A7ois_Lambert" title="Joseph-François Lambert">Joseph-François Lambert</a>, a French adventurer and slave trader, with whom then-Prince <a href="/wiki/Radama_II" title="Radama II">Radama II</a> signed a controversial trade agreement termed the Lambert Charter. Succeeding his mother, Radama II attempted to relax the queen's stringent policies, but was <a href="/wiki/Radama_II#Assassination_plot" title="Radama II">overthrown</a> two years later by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Rainivoninahitriniony" title="Rainivoninahitriniony">Rainivoninahitriniony</a> and an alliance of <i><a href="/wiki/Andriana" title="Andriana">Andriana</a></i> (noble) and <i><a href="/wiki/Hova_(Madagascar)" title="Hova (Madagascar)">Hova</a></i> (commoner) courtiers, who sought to end the absolute power of the monarch.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the coup, the courtiers offered Radama's queen, <a href="/wiki/Rasoherina" title="Rasoherina">Rasoherina</a>, the opportunity to rule, if she would accept a power sharing arrangement with the Prime Minister: a new social contract that would be sealed by a political marriage between them.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Queen Rasoherina accepted, first marrying Rainivoninahitriniony, then later deposing him and marrying his brother, Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Rainilaiarivony" title="Rainilaiarivony">Rainilaiarivony</a>, who would go on to marry Queen <a href="/wiki/Ranavalona_II" title="Ranavalona II">Ranavalona II</a> and Queen <a href="/wiki/Ranavalona_III" title="Ranavalona III">Ranavalona III</a> in succession.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Over the course of Rainilaiarivony's 31-year tenure as prime minister, numerous policies were adopted to modernize and consolidate the power of the central government.<sup id="cite_ref-TA910_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TA910-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Schools were constructed throughout the island and attendance was made mandatory. Army organization was improved and British consultants were employed to train and professionalize soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-FFO522_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FFO522-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Polygamy" title="Polygamy">Polygamy</a> was outlawed and Christianity, declared the official religion of the court in 1869, was adopted alongside traditional beliefs among a growing portion of the populace.<sup id="cite_ref-TA910_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TA910-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Legal codes were reformed on the basis of British <a href="/wiki/Common_law" title="Common law">common law</a> and three European-style courts were established in the capital city.<sup id="cite_ref-FFO522_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FFO522-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> In his joint role as Commander-in-Chief, Rainilaiarivony also successfully ensured the defense of Madagascar against several French colonial incursions.<sup id="cite_ref-FFO522_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FFO522-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="French_colonization">French colonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: French colonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Protectorate" title="Malagasy Protectorate">Malagasy Protectorate</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_Madagascar" title="French Madagascar">French Madagascar</a></div> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_in_World_War_II" title="Madagascar in World War II">Madagascar in World War II</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg/170px-LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="256" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg/255px-LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg/340px-LaGuerreAMadagascar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5300" data-file-height="7976" /></a><figcaption>A French poster about the <a href="/wiki/Franco-Hova_Wars" title="Franco-Hova Wars">Franco-Hova War</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Primarily on the basis that the Lambert Charter had not been respected, France invaded Madagascar in 1883 in what became known as the first <a href="/wiki/Franco-Hova_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Franco-Hova War">Franco-Hova War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Boogaerde_p7_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boogaerde_p7-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> At the end of the war, Madagascar ceded the northern port town of <a href="/wiki/Antsiranana" title="Antsiranana">Antsiranana</a> (Diego Suarez) to France and paid 560,000 francs to Lambert's heirs.<sup id="cite_ref-Randier_p400_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Randier_p400-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In 1890, the British accepted the full formal imposition of a French <a href="/wiki/Protectorate" title="Protectorate">protectorate</a> on the island, but French authority was not acknowledged by the government of Madagascar. To force capitulation, the French bombarded and occupied the harbor of <a href="/wiki/Toamasina" title="Toamasina">Toamasina</a> on the east coast, and <a href="/wiki/Mahajanga" title="Mahajanga">Mahajanga</a> on the west coast, in December 1894 and January 1895 respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-Disease_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Disease-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A French military <a href="/wiki/Flying_column" title="Flying column">flying column</a> then marched toward Antananarivo, losing many men to malaria and other diseases. Reinforcements came from <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">Sub-Saharan Africa</a>. Upon reaching the city in September 1895, the column bombarded the royal palace with heavy artillery, causing heavy casualties and leading Queen Ranavalona III to surrender.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> France annexed Madagascar in 1896 and declared the island a colony the following year, dissolving the Merina monarchy and sending the royal family into exile on <a href="/wiki/R%C3%A9union" title="Réunion">Réunion Island</a> and to Algeria. A two-year <a href="/wiki/Menalamba_rebellion" title="Menalamba rebellion">resistance movement</a> organized in response to the French capture of the royal palace was effectively put down at the end of 1897.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The conquest was followed by ten years of civil war, due to the Menalamba insurrection. The "pacification" carried out by the French administration lasted more than fifteen years, in response to the rural guerrillas scattered throughout the country. In total, the repression of this resistance to colonial conquest caused several tens of thousands of Malagasy victims.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under colonial rule, plantations were established for the production of a variety of export crops.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Slavery" title="Slavery">Slavery</a> was abolished in 1896 and approximately 500,000 slaves were freed; many remained in their former masters' homes as servants<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> or as sharecroppers; in many parts of the island strong discriminatory views against slave descendants are still held today.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Wide paved boulevards and gathering places were constructed in the capital city of Antananarivo<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> and the Rova palace compound was turned into a museum.<sup id="cite_ref-Frémigacci_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frémigacci-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Additional schools were built, particularly in rural and coastal areas where the schools of the Merina had not reached. Education became mandatory between the ages of 6 and 13 and focused primarily on French language and practical skills.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg/220px-29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg/330px-29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg/440px-29_Mars_1947_Monument.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>National monument in <a href="/wiki/Moramanga" title="Moramanga">Moramanga</a> commemorating the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Uprising" title="Malagasy Uprising">Malagasy Uprising</a> on 29 March 1947. Between 11,000 and 90,000 Malagasy died during the uprising which lasted nearly two years.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Huge mining and forestry concessions were granted to large companies. Native chiefs loyal to the French administration were also granted part of the land. Forced labor was introduced in favor of the French companies and peasants were encouraged, through taxation, to work for wages (especially in the colonial concessions) to the detriment of small individual farms. However, the colonial period was accompanied by movements fighting for independence: the Menalamba, the Vy Vato Sakelika, the Democratic Movement for Malagasy Renovation (MDRM). In 1927, major demonstrations were organized in Antananarivo, notably on the initiative of the communist activist François Vittori, who was imprisoned as a result. The 1930s saw the Malagasy anti-colonial movement gain further momentum. Malagasy trade unionism began to appear underground and the Communist Party of the Madagascar region was formed. But in 1939, all the organizations were dissolved by the administration of the colony, which opted for the Vichy regime. The MDRM was accused by the colonial regime of being at the origin of the 1947 insurrection and was pursued by violent repression.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Merina royal tradition of <a href="/wiki/Corv%C3%A9e" title="Corvée">taxes paid in the form of labor</a> was continued under the French and used to construct a railway and roads linking key coastal cities to Antananarivo.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Malagasy troops fought for France in <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In the 1930s, <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi</a> political thinkers developed the <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_Plan" title="Madagascar Plan">Madagascar Plan</a> that had identified the island as a potential site for the deportation of Europe's Jews.<sup id="cite_ref-contemplation_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-contemplation-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>, the island was the site of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Madagascar" title="Battle of Madagascar">Battle of Madagascar</a> between the <a href="/wiki/Vichy_France" title="Vichy France">Vichy French</a> and an <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II" title="Allies of World War II">Allied</a> expeditionary force.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The occupation of France during the Second World War tarnished the prestige of the colonial administration in Madagascar and galvanized the growing independence movement, leading to the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Uprising" title="Malagasy Uprising">Malagasy Uprising</a> of 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> This movement led the French to establish reformed institutions in 1956 under the <i><a href="/wiki/Loi_Cadre" class="mw-redirect" title="Loi Cadre">Loi Cadre</a></i> (Overseas Reform Act), and Madagascar moved peacefully towards independence.<sup id="cite_ref-Kitchen_1962,_p._256_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kitchen_1962,_p._256-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Republic" title="Malagasy Republic">Malagasy Republic</a> was proclaimed on 14 October 1958, as an <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_entity" class="mw-redirect" title="Autonomous entity">autonomous</a> state within the <a href="/wiki/French_Community" title="French Community">French Community</a>. A period of provisional government ended with the adoption of a constitution in 1959 and full independence on 26 June 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Independent_state">Independent state</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Independent state">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Republic" title="Malagasy Republic">Malagasy Republic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Madagascar" title="Democratic Republic of Madagascar">Democratic Republic of Madagascar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Third_Republic_of_Madagascar" title="Third Republic of Madagascar">Third Republic of Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg/170px-Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg/255px-Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg/340px-Philibert_Tsiranana_1962.jpg 2x" data-file-width="531" data-file-height="705" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Philibert_Tsiranana" title="Philibert Tsiranana">Philibert Tsiranana</a>, the first <a href="/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Presidents of Madagascar">president of Madagascar</a> (1960–1972)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Since regaining independence, Madagascar has transitioned through four republics with corresponding revisions to its constitution. The <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Republic" title="Malagasy Republic">First Republic</a> (1960–72), under the leadership of French-appointed President <a href="/wiki/Philibert_Tsiranana" title="Philibert Tsiranana">Philibert Tsiranana</a>, was characterized by a continuation of strong economic and political ties to France. Many high-level technical positions were filled by French expatriates, and French teachers, textbooks and curricula continued to be used in schools around the country. Popular resentment over Tsiranana's tolerance for this "neo-colonial" arrangement inspired a <a href="/wiki/Rotaka" title="Rotaka">series of farmer and student protests</a> that overturned his administration in 1972.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Gabriel_Ramanantsoa" title="Gabriel Ramanantsoa">Gabriel Ramanantsoa</a>, a major general in the army, was appointed interim president and prime minister that same year, but low public approval forced him to step down in 1975. Colonel <a href="/wiki/Richard_Ratsimandrava" title="Richard Ratsimandrava">Richard Ratsimandrava</a>, appointed to succeed him, was assassinated six days into his tenure. General <a href="/wiki/Gilles_Andriamahazo" title="Gilles Andriamahazo">Gilles Andriamahazo</a> ruled after Ratsimandrava for four months before being replaced by another military appointee: Vice Admiral <a href="/wiki/Didier_Ratsiraka" title="Didier Ratsiraka">Didier Ratsiraka</a>, who ushered in the Socialist-Marxist <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Madagascar" title="Democratic Republic of Madagascar">Second Republic</a> that ran under his tenure from 1975 to 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This period saw a political alignment with the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Bloc" title="Eastern Bloc">Eastern Bloc</a> countries and a shift toward economic insularity. These policies, coupled with economic pressures stemming from the <a href="/wiki/1973_oil_crisis" title="1973 oil crisis">1973 oil crisis</a>, resulted in the rapid collapse of Madagascar's economy and a sharp decline in living standards,<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> and the country had become completely bankrupt by 1979. The Ratsiraka administration accepted the conditions of transparency, anti-corruption measures and free market policies imposed by the International Monetary Fund, <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> and various bilateral donors in exchange for their bailout of the nation's broken economy.<sup id="cite_ref-ISS_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ISS-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ratsiraka's dwindling popularity in the late 1980s reached a critical point in 1991 when presidential guards opened fire on unarmed protesters during a rally. Within two months, a transitional government had been established under the leadership of <a href="/wiki/Albert_Zafy" title="Albert Zafy">Albert Zafy</a> (1993–96), who went on to win the 1992 presidential elections and inaugurate the <a href="/wiki/Third_Republic_of_Madagascar" title="Third Republic of Madagascar">Third Republic</a> (1992–2010).<sup id="cite_ref-crisisgroup_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crisisgroup-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The new <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Madagascar" title="Constitution of Madagascar">Madagascar constitution</a> established a multi-party democracy and a separation of powers that placed significant control in the hands of the National Assembly. The new constitution also emphasized human rights, social and political freedoms, and free trade.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Zafy's term, however, was marred by economic decline, allegations of corruption, and his introduction of legislation to give himself greater powers. He was consequently impeached in 1996, and an interim president, <a href="/wiki/Norbert_Ratsirahonana" title="Norbert Ratsirahonana">Norbert Ratsirahonana</a>, was appointed for the three months prior to the next presidential election. Ratsiraka was then voted back into power on a platform of decentralization and economic reforms for a second term which lasted from 1996 to 2001.<sup id="cite_ref-ISS_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ISS-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The contested <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_presidential_election,_2001" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy presidential election, 2001">2001 presidential elections</a> in which then-mayor of Antananarivo, Marc Ravalomanana, eventually emerged victorious, caused a seven-month standoff in 2002 between supporters of Ravalomanana and Ratsiraka. The negative economic impact of the political crisis was gradually overcome by Ravalomanana's progressive economic and political policies, which encouraged investments in education and ecotourism, facilitated foreign direct investment, and cultivated trading partnerships both regionally and internationally. National GDP grew at an average rate of 7&#160;percent per year under his administration. In the latter half of his second term, Ravalomanana was criticised by domestic and international observers who accused him of increasing authoritarianism and corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-ISS_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ISS-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Opposition leader and then-mayor of Antananarivo, <a href="/wiki/Andry_Rajoelina" title="Andry Rajoelina">Andry Rajoelina</a>, led a <a href="/wiki/2009_Malagasy_political_crisis" title="2009 Malagasy political crisis">movement in early 2009</a> in which Ravalomanana was pushed from power in an unconstitutional process widely condemned as a <i>coup d'état</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In March 2009, Rajoelina was declared by the Supreme Court as the President of the <a href="/wiki/High_Transitional_Authority" title="High Transitional Authority">High Transitional Authority</a>, an interim governing body responsible for moving the country toward presidential elections. In 2010, a new constitution was <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_constitutional_referendum,_2010" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy constitutional referendum, 2010">adopted by referendum</a>, establishing a Fourth Republic, which sustained the democratic, multi-party structure established in the previous constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-crisisgroup_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crisisgroup-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hery_Rajaonarimampianina" title="Hery Rajaonarimampianina">Hery Rajaonarimampianina</a> was declared the winner of the <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_general_election,_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy general election, 2013">2013 presidential election</a>, which the international community deemed fair and transparent.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2018 the first round of the <a href="/wiki/2018_Malagasy_presidential_election" title="2018 Malagasy presidential election">presidential election</a> was held on 7 November and the second round was held on 10 December. Three former presidents and the most recent president were the main candidates of the elections. Former president Andry Rajoelina won the second round of the elections. He was previously president from 2009 to 2014. Former president Marc Ravalomana lost the second round and he did not accept the results because of allegations of fraud. Ravalomana was president from 2002 to 2009. The most recent president Hery Rajaonarimampianina received very modest support in the first round. In January 2019 the High Constitutional Court declared Rajoelina as the winner of the elections and the new president.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
In June 2019 <a href="/wiki/2019_Malagasy_parliamentary_election" title="2019 Malagasy parliamentary election">parliamentary elections</a> the party of president Andry Rajoelina won absolute majority of the seats of the National Assembly. It received 84 seats and the supporters of former president Ravalomana got only 16 seats of 151 seats of the National Assembly. 51 seats of deputies were independent or represented small parties. President Rajoelina could rule as a <a href="/wiki/Strongman_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Strongman (politics)">strongman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Madagascar" title="Geography of Madagascar">Geography of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geology_of_Madagascar" title="Geology of Madagascar">Geology of Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Madagascar_topo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Madagascar_topo.jpg/290px-Madagascar_topo.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Madagascar_topo.jpg/435px-Madagascar_topo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e9/Madagascar_topo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="540" data-file-height="540" /></a><figcaption>Land coverage (left) and topographical (right) maps of Madagascar</figcaption></figure>
<p>At 592,800 square kilometres (228,900&#160;sq&#160;mi),<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar is the world's 46th <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area" title="List of countries and dependencies by area">largest country</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-cia_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> the second-largest <a href="/wiki/List_of_island_countries" title="List of island countries">island country</a><sup id="cite_ref-world-atlas_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-world-atlas-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/List_of_islands_by_area" title="List of islands by area">fourth-largest island</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The country lies mostly between latitudes <a href="/wiki/12th_parallel_south" title="12th parallel south">12°S</a> and <a href="/wiki/26th_parallel_south" title="26th parallel south">26°S</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/43rd_meridian_east" title="43rd meridian east">43°E</a> and <a href="/wiki/51st_meridian_east" title="51st meridian east">51°E</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-georeport_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-georeport-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Neighboring islands include the French territory of <a href="/wiki/R%C3%A9union" title="Réunion">Réunion</a> and the country of <a href="/wiki/Mauritius" title="Mauritius">Mauritius</a> to the east, as well as the state of <a href="/wiki/Comoros" title="Comoros">Comoros</a> and the French territory of <a href="/wiki/Mayotte" title="Mayotte">Mayotte</a> to the north west. The nearest mainland state is <a href="/wiki/Mozambique" title="Mozambique">Mozambique</a>, located to the west.
</p><p>The prehistoric breakup of the supercontinent <a href="/wiki/Gondwana" title="Gondwana">Gondwana</a> resulted in the separation of East Gondwana (comprising Madagascar, Antarctica, Australia and the Indian subcontinent) and West Gondwana (Africa–South America) during the <a href="/wiki/Jurassic" title="Jurassic">Jurassic</a> period, around 185&#160;million years ago. The Indo-Madagascar landmass separated from Antarctica and Australia around 125 million years ago<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> and Madagascar separated from the Indian landmass about 84–92&#160;million years ago during the Late <a href="/wiki/Cretaceous" title="Cretaceous">Cretaceous</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> This long history of separation from other continents has allowed plants and animals on the island to evolve in relative isolation. Along the length of the eastern coast runs a narrow and steep <a href="/wiki/Escarpment" title="Escarpment">escarpment</a> containing much of the island's remaining tropical <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_lowland_forests" title="Madagascar lowland forests">lowland forest</a>. To the west of this ridge lies a <a href="/wiki/Plateau" title="Plateau">plateau</a> in the center of the island ranging in altitude from 750 to 1,500&#160;m (2,460 to 4,920&#160;ft) above sea level. These <a href="/wiki/Central_Highlands_(Madagascar)" title="Central Highlands (Madagascar)">central highlands</a>, traditionally the homeland of the <a href="/wiki/Merina_people" title="Merina people">Merina people</a> and the location of their historic capital at <a href="/wiki/Antananarivo" title="Antananarivo">Antananarivo</a>, are the most densely populated part of the island and are characterized by terraced, rice-growing valleys lying between grassy hills and patches of the <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_subhumid_forests" title="Madagascar subhumid forests">subhumid forests</a> that formerly covered the highland region. To the west of the highlands, the increasingly arid terrain gradually slopes down to the <a href="/wiki/Mozambique_Channel" title="Mozambique Channel">Mozambique Channel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_mangroves" title="Madagascar mangroves">mangrove swamps</a> along the coast.<sup id="cite_ref-endemicstats_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-endemicstats-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madagascar's highest peaks rise from three prominent highland <a href="/wiki/Massif" title="Massif">massifs</a>: <a href="/wiki/Maromokotro" title="Maromokotro">Maromokotro</a> 2,876&#160;m (9,436&#160;ft) in the <a href="/wiki/Tsaratanana_Massif" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsaratanana Massif">Tsaratanana Massif</a> is the island's highest point, followed by Boby Peak 2,658&#160;m (8,720&#160;ft) in the <a href="/wiki/Andringitra_Massif" title="Andringitra Massif">Andringitra Massif</a>, and Tsiafajavona 2,643&#160;m (8,671&#160;ft) in the <a href="/wiki/Ankaratra" title="Ankaratra">Ankaratra</a> Massif. To the east, the <i><a href="/wiki/Canal_des_Pangalanes" title="Canal des Pangalanes">Canal des Pangalanes</a></i> is a chain of human-made and natural lakes connected by canals built by the French just inland from the east coast and running parallel to it for some 600&#160;km (370&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The western and southern sides, which lie in the <a href="/wiki/Rain_shadow" title="Rain shadow">rain shadow</a> of the central highlands, are home to <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_dry_deciduous_forests" title="Madagascar dry deciduous forests">dry deciduous forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_spiny_forests" title="Madagascar spiny forests">spiny forests</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Deserts_and_xeric_shrublands" title="Deserts and xeric shrublands">deserts and xeric shrublands</a>. Due to their lower population densities, Madagascar's dry deciduous forests have been better preserved than the eastern rain forests or the original woodlands of the central plateau. The western coast features many protected harbors, but silting is a major problem caused by sediment from the high levels of inland erosion carried by rivers crossing the broad western plains.<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg/290px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="209" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg/435px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg/580px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MDG_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1181" data-file-height="850" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Köppen climate classification</a> map of Madagascar</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Madagascar#Climate" title="Geography of Madagascar">Geography of Madagascar §&#160;Climate</a></div>
<p>The combination of southeastern <a href="/wiki/Trade_winds" title="Trade winds">trade winds</a> and northwestern <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoons</a> produces a hot rainy season (November–April) with frequently destructive <a href="/wiki/Cyclone" title="Cyclone">cyclones</a>, and a relatively cooler dry season (May–October). Rain clouds originating over the Indian Ocean discharge much of their moisture over the island's eastern coast; the heavy precipitation supports the area's <a href="/wiki/Rainforest" title="Rainforest">rainforest</a> ecosystem. The central highlands are both drier and cooler while the west is drier still, and a <a href="/wiki/Semi-arid_climate" title="Semi-arid climate">semi-arid climate</a> prevails in the southwest and southern interior of the island.<sup id="cite_ref-endemicstats_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-endemicstats-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Tropical_cyclone" title="Tropical cyclone">Tropical cyclones</a> cause damage to infrastructure and local economies as well as loss of life.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In 2004, <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Gafilo" title="Cyclone Gafilo">Cyclone Gafilo</a> became the strongest cyclone ever recorded to hit Madagascar. The storm killed 172 people, left 214,260 homeless<sup id="cite_ref-gafilo_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gafilo-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> and caused more than US$250&#160;million in damage.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> In February 2022, <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Batsirai" title="Cyclone Batsirai">Cyclone Batsirai</a> killed at least 10 people weeks after <a href="/wiki/Cyclone_Ana" title="Cyclone Ana">Cyclone Ana</a> killed 55 and displaced 130,000 people on the island.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecology">Ecology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Ecology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Madagascar" title="Wildlife of Madagascar">Wildlife of Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Flora_of_Madagascar" title="Flora of Madagascar">Flora of Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fauna_of_Madagascar" title="Fauna of Madagascar">Fauna of Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_Madagascar" title="Agriculture in Madagascar">Agriculture in Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ecoregions_of_Madagascar" title="Ecoregions of Madagascar">Ecoregions of Madagascar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Madagascar" title="List of World Heritage Sites in Madagascar">List of World Heritage Sites in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg/220px-Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg/330px-Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg/440px-Angraecum_sesquipedale_Orchi_4.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="1280" /></a><figcaption>Comet orchid (<i><a href="/wiki/Angraecum_sesquipedale" title="Angraecum sesquipedale">Angraecum sesquipedale</a></i>), the flowers of this orchid have a very long spur and are pollinated by a species of <a href="/wiki/Sphingidae" title="Sphingidae">hawkmoth</a> with a proboscis of matching length.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As a result of the island's long isolation from neighboring continents, Madagascar is home to various <a href="/wiki/Endemic" class="mw-redirect" title="Endemic">endemic</a> plants and animals found nowhere else on Earth.<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-tattersall_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tattersall-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Approximately 90% of all plant and animal species found in Madagascar are <a href="/wiki/Endemism" title="Endemism">endemic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> This distinctive ecology has led some ecologists to refer to Madagascar as the "eighth continent",<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> and the island has been classified by <a href="/wiki/Conservation_International" title="Conservation International">Conservation International</a> as a biodiversity hotspot.<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar is classed as one of 17 <a href="/wiki/Megadiverse_countries" title="Megadiverse countries">megadiverse countries</a>. The country is home to seven terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_lowland_forests" title="Madagascar lowland forests">Madagascar lowland forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_subhumid_forests" title="Madagascar subhumid forests">Madagascar subhumid forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_dry_deciduous_forests" title="Madagascar dry deciduous forests">Madagascar dry deciduous forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_ericoid_thickets" title="Madagascar ericoid thickets">Madagascar ericoid thickets</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_spiny_forests" title="Madagascar spiny forests">Madagascar spiny forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_succulent_woodlands" title="Madagascar succulent woodlands">Madagascar succulent woodlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_mangroves" title="Madagascar mangroves">Madagascar mangroves</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>More than 80&#160;percent of Madagascar's 14,883 <a href="/wiki/Flora_of_Madagascar" title="Flora of Madagascar">plant species</a> are found nowhere else in the world, including five plant families.<sup id="cite_ref-endemism_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-endemism-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The family <i><a href="/wiki/Didiereaceae" title="Didiereaceae">Didiereaceae</a></i>, composed of four genera and 11 species, is limited to the <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_spiny_thickets" class="mw-redirect" title="Madagascar spiny thickets">spiny forests</a> of southwestern Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-endemicstats_97-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-endemicstats-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Four-fifths of the world's <i><a href="/wiki/Pachypodium" title="Pachypodium">Pachypodium</a></i> species are endemic to the island.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Three-fourths<sup id="cite_ref-B2011plant_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-B2011plant-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> of Madagascar's 860<sup id="cite_ref-endemism_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-endemism-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Orchidaceae" class="mw-redirect" title="Orchidaceae">orchid</a> species are found here alone, as are six of the world's nine <a href="/wiki/Adansonia" title="Adansonia">baobab</a> species.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> The island is home to around 170 <a href="/wiki/Arecaceae" title="Arecaceae">palm</a> species, three times as many as on all of mainland Africa; 165 of them are endemic.<sup id="cite_ref-B2011plant_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-B2011plant-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Many native plant species are used as herbal remedies for a variety of afflictions. The drugs <a href="/wiki/Vinblastine" title="Vinblastine">vinblastine</a><sup id="cite_ref-MoleculesReview_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MoleculesReview-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Vincristine" title="Vincristine">vincristine</a><sup id="cite_ref-MoleculesReview_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MoleculesReview-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> are <a href="/wiki/Vinca_alkaloid" title="Vinca alkaloid"><i>vinca</i> alkaloids</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> used to treat <a href="/wiki/Hodgkin_lymphoma" title="Hodgkin lymphoma">Hodgkin lymphoma</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Leukemia" title="Leukemia">leukemia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> and other cancers,<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> were derived from the <a href="/wiki/Catharanthus" title="Catharanthus">Madagascar periwinkle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-periw_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-periw-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Ravenala" title="Ravenala">traveler's palm</a>, known locally as <i>ravinala</i><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> and endemic to the eastern rain forests,<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> is highly iconic of Madagascar and is featured in the national emblem as well as the <a href="/wiki/Air_Madagascar" title="Air Madagascar">Air Madagascar</a> logo.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:OaklandZooLemurs.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Two ring-tailed lemurs curled up together" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/OaklandZooLemurs.jpg/220px-OaklandZooLemurs.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/OaklandZooLemurs.jpg/330px-OaklandZooLemurs.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/OaklandZooLemurs.jpg/440px-OaklandZooLemurs.jpg 2x" data-file-width="899" data-file-height="599" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Ring-tailed_lemur" title="Ring-tailed lemur">ring-tailed lemur</a> is one of over 100 known species and subspecies of lemur found only in Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-2009Mittermeier_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2009Mittermeier-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Like its flora, Madagascar's fauna is diverse and exhibits a high rate of endemism. Lemurs have been characterized as "Madagascar's flagship mammal species" by Conservation International.<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> In the absence of monkeys and other competitors, these <a href="/wiki/Primate" title="Primate">primates</a> have adapted to a wide range of habitats and diversified into numerous species. As of 2012<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, there were officially <a href="/wiki/List_of_lemur_species" title="List of lemur species">103 species and subspecies of lemur</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-lemurextinction_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lemurextinction-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> 39 of which were described by zoologists between 2000 and 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-Mittermeier2008_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mittermeier2008-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> They are almost all classified as rare, vulnerable, or endangered. At least 17 species of lemur have become extinct since humans arrived on Madagascar, all of which were larger than the surviving lemur species.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
