</p><p>A number of other mammals, including the catlike <a href="/wiki/Fossa_(animal)" title="Fossa (animal)">fossa</a>, are endemic to Madagascar. Over 300 species of birds have been recorded on the island, of which over 60&#160;percent (including four families and 42 genera) are endemic.<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> The few families and genera of <a href="/wiki/Reptile" title="Reptile">reptiles</a> that have reached Madagascar have diversified into more than 260 species, with over 90&#160;percent of these being endemic<sup id="cite_ref-Okajima_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Okajima-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> (including one endemic family).<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> The island is home to two-thirds of the world's <a href="/wiki/Chameleon" title="Chameleon">chameleon</a> species,<sup id="cite_ref-Okajima_128-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Okajima-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> including the <a href="/wiki/Brookesia_nana" title="Brookesia nana">smallest known</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Endemic fish of Madagascar include two families, 15 genera and over 100 species, primarily inhabiting the island's freshwater lakes and rivers. Although invertebrates remain poorly studied in Madagascar, researchers have found high rates of endemism among the known species. All 651 species of terrestrial snail are endemic, as are a majority of the island's butterflies, <a href="/wiki/Scarabaeidae" title="Scarabaeidae">scarab beetles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neuroptera" title="Neuroptera">lacewings</a>, spiders, and dragonflies.<sup id="cite_ref-CIHotSpot_102-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIHotSpot-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environmental_issues">Environmental issues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Environmental issues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Deforestation_in_Madagascar" title="Deforestation in Madagascar">Deforestation in Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Illegal_logging_in_Madagascar" title="Illegal logging in Madagascar">Illegal logging in Madagascar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Madagascar" title="Climate change in Madagascar">Climate change in Madagascar</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:204px;max-width:204px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Manantenina_bushfire.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Burning Malagasy rainforest" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Manantenina_bushfire.jpg/200px-Manantenina_bushfire.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Manantenina_bushfire.jpg/300px-Manantenina_bushfire.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Manantenina_bushfire.jpg/400px-Manantenina_bushfire.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2166" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Madagascar_erosion.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A vast, red soil gully caused by erosion" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Madagascar_erosion.jpg/200px-Madagascar_erosion.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Madagascar_erosion.jpg/300px-Madagascar_erosion.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Madagascar_erosion.jpg/400px-Madagascar_erosion.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption">Tavy (<a href="/wiki/Slash-and-burn" title="Slash-and-burn">slash-and-burn</a>) destruction of native forest habitat is widespread (top), causing massive erosion (bottom).</div></div></div></div>
<p>Madagascar's varied fauna and flora are endangered by human activity.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Since the arrival of humans around 2,350&#160;years ago, Madagascar has lost more than 90&#160;percent of its original forest.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> This forest loss is largely fueled by <i>tavy</i> ("fat"), a traditional <a href="/wiki/Slash-and-burn" title="Slash-and-burn">slash-and-burn</a> agricultural practice imported to Madagascar by the earliest settlers.<sup id="cite_ref-Gade_1996_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gade_1996-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Malagasy farmers embrace and perpetuate the practice not only for its practical benefits as an agricultural technique, but for its cultural associations with prosperity, health and venerated ancestral custom (<i>fomba malagasy</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> As human population density rose on the island, deforestation accelerated beginning around 1,400 years ago.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> By the 16th century, the central highlands had been largely cleared of their original forests.<sup id="cite_ref-Gade_1996_44-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gade_1996-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> More recent contributors to the loss of forest cover include the growth in cattle herd size since their introduction around 1,000 years ago, a continued reliance on charcoal as a fuel for cooking, and the increased prominence of coffee as a <a href="/wiki/Cash_crop" title="Cash crop">cash crop</a> over the past century.<sup id="cite_ref-spittingwind_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spittingwind-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 4.63/10, ranking it 119th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a conservative estimate, about 40&#160;percent of the island's original forest cover was lost from the 1950s to 2000, with a thinning of remaining forest areas by 80&#160;percent.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> In addition to traditional agricultural practice, wildlife conservation is challenged by the illicit harvesting of protected forests, as well as the state-sanctioned harvesting of precious woods within national parks. Although banned by then-President <a href="/wiki/Marc_Ravalomanana" title="Marc Ravalomanana">Marc Ravalomanana</a> from 2000 to 2009, the collection of small quantities of precious timber from national parks was re-authorized in January 2009 and dramatically intensified under the administration of <a href="/wiki/Andry_Rajoelina" title="Andry Rajoelina">Andry Rajoelina</a> as a key source of state revenues to offset cuts in donor support following Ravalomanana's ousting.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Invasive_species"></span>Invasive species have likewise been introduced by human populations. Following the 2014 discovery in Madagascar of the <a href="/wiki/Duttaphrynus_melanostictus" title="Duttaphrynus melanostictus">Asian common toad</a>, a relative of a toad species that has severely harmed wildlife in Australia since the 1930s, researchers warned the toad could "wreak havoc on the country's unique fauna."<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Habitat destruction and hunting have threatened many of Madagascar's endemic species or driven them to extinction. The island's <a href="/wiki/Elephant_bird" title="Elephant bird">elephant birds</a>, a family of endemic giant <a href="/wiki/Ratite" title="Ratite">ratites</a>, became extinct in the 17th century or earlier, most probably because of human hunting of adult birds and poaching of their large eggs for food.<sup id="cite_ref-Davies_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davies-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Numerous <a href="/wiki/Subfossil_lemur" title="Subfossil lemur">giant lemur</a> species vanished with the arrival of human settlers to the island, while others became extinct over the course of the centuries as a growing human population put greater pressures on lemur habitats and, among some populations, increased the rate of lemur hunting for food.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> A July 2012 assessment found that the exploitation of natural resources since 2009 has had dire consequences for the island's wildlife: 90&#160;percent of lemur species were found to be threatened with extinction, the highest proportion of any mammalian group. Of these, 23 species were classified as critically endangered. A 2023 study published in <i><a href="/wiki/Nature_Communications" title="Nature Communications">Nature Communications</a></i> found that 120 of the 219 mammal species only found on Madagascar are threatened with extinction.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2003, Ravalomanana announced the Durban Vision, an initiative to more than triple the island's <a href="/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Madagascar" title="Protected areas of Madagascar">protected natural areas</a> to over 60,000&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (23,000&#160;sq&#160;mi) or 10&#160;percent of Madagascar's land surface. As of 2011<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, areas protected by the state included five Strict Nature Reserves (<i>Réserves Naturelles Intégrales</i>), 21 Wildlife Reserves (<i>Réserves Spéciales</i>) and 21 National Parks (<i>Parcs Nationaux</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> In 2007 six of the national parks were declared a joint <a href="/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Madagascar" title="List of World Heritage Sites in Madagascar">World Heritage Site</a> under the name <a href="/wiki/Rainforests_of_the_Atsinanana" title="Rainforests of the Atsinanana">Rainforests of the Atsinanana</a>. These parks are <a href="/wiki/Marojejy_National_Park" title="Marojejy National Park">Marojejy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Masoala_National_Park" title="Masoala National Park">Masoala</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ranomafana_National_Park" title="Ranomafana National Park">Ranomafana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zahamena_National_Park" title="Zahamena National Park">Zahamena</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andohahela_National_Park" title="Andohahela National Park">Andohahela</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andringitra_National_Park" title="Andringitra National Park">Andringitra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Local timber merchants are harvesting scarce species of rosewood trees from protected rainforests within Marojejy National Park and exporting the wood to China for the production of luxury furniture and musical instruments.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mid-2021 marked the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_Madagascar_famine" title="2021–2022 Madagascar famine">2021–2022 Madagascar famine</a> which, due to a severe drought, caused hundreds of thousands of people to face <a href="/wiki/Food_security" title="Food security">food insecurity</a> and over one million people were on the verge of a <a href="/wiki/Famine" title="Famine">famine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A 2022 analysis found that the expected costs for Madagascar, to adapt to and avert the environmental consequences of <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Madagascar" title="Climate change in Madagascar">climate change</a>, are going to be high.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Structure">Structure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Structure">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Government of Madagascar">Government of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Madagascar" title="Cabinet of Madagascar">Cabinet of Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antananarivo05.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Antananarivo05.jpg/220px-Antananarivo05.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Antananarivo05.jpg/330px-Antananarivo05.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Antananarivo05.jpg/440px-Antananarivo05.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2288" data-file-height="1712" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Antananarivo" title="Antananarivo">Antananarivo</a> is the political and economic capital of Madagascar.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Madagascar is a <a href="/wiki/Semi-presidential_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Semi-presidential system">semi-presidential</a> <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democratic</a> <a href="/wiki/Diversity_(politics)" title="Diversity (politics)">multi-party</a> republic, wherein the popularly elected president is the head of state and selects a <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Madagascar" title="Prime Minister of Madagascar">prime minister</a>, who recommends candidates to the president to form his cabinet of ministers. According to the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Madagascar" title="Constitution of Madagascar">constitution</a>, executive power is exercised by the government while legislative power is vested in the ministerial cabinet,<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Senate of Madagascar">Senate</a> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Madagascar">National Assembly</a>, although in reality these two latter bodies have very little power or legislative role. The constitution establishes independent executive, legislative and judicial branches and mandates a popularly elected president limited to three five-year terms.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The public directly elects the president and the 127 members of the National Assembly to five-year terms. All 33 members of the Senate serve six-year terms, with 22 senators elected by local officials and 11 appointed by the president. The last National Assembly election was held on 20 December 2013<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and the last Senate election was held on 30 December 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the local level, the island's 22 provinces are administered by a governor and provincial council. Provinces are further subdivided into regions and communes. The judiciary is modeled on the French system, with a High Constitutional Court, High Court of Justice, Supreme Court, Court of Appeals, criminal tribunals, and tribunals of first instance.<sup id="cite_ref-justiceMada_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-justiceMada-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> The courts, which adhere to <a href="/wiki/Civil_law_(legal_system)" title="Civil law (legal system)">civil law</a>, lack the capacity to quickly and transparently try the cases in the judicial system, often forcing defendants to pass lengthy pretrial detentions in unsanitary and overcrowded prisons.<sup id="cite_ref-mediainfo_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mediainfo-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Antananarivo is the administrative capital and largest city of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> It is located in the highlands region, near the geographic center of the island. King Andrianjaka founded Antananarivo as the capital of his Imerina Kingdom around 1610 or 1625 upon the site of a captured Vazimba capital on the hilltop of <a href="/wiki/Twelve_sacred_hills_of_Imerina#Hill_of_Analamanga" title="Twelve sacred hills of Imerina">Analamanga</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-vazimbadjp_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vazimbadjp-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> As Merina dominance expanded over neighboring Malagasy peoples in the early 19th century to establish the Kingdom of Madagascar, Antananarivo became the center of administration for virtually the entire island. In 1896 the French colonizers of Madagascar adopted the Merina capital as their center of colonial administration. The city remained the capital of Madagascar after regaining independence in 1960. In 2017, the capital's population was estimated at 1,391,433 inhabitants.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> The next largest cities are <a href="/wiki/Antsirabe" title="Antsirabe">Antsirabe</a> (500,000), <a href="/wiki/Toamasina" title="Toamasina">Toamasina</a> (450,000) and <a href="/wiki/Mahajanga" title="Mahajanga">Mahajanga</a> (400,000).<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Politics">Politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Madagascar" title="Politics of Madagascar">Politics of Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Madagascar" title="Foreign relations of Madagascar">Foreign relations of Madagascar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Madagascar" title="Human rights in Madagascar">Human rights in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg/190px-Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg/285px-Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg/380px-Andry_Rajoelina_greeting_crowd.jpg 2x" data-file-width="716" data-file-height="839" /></a><figcaption>Madagascar's President <a href="/wiki/Andry_Rajoelina" title="Andry Rajoelina">Andry Rajoelina</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Since Madagascar gained independence from France in 1960, the island's political transitions have been marked by numerous popular protests, several disputed elections, an impeachment, two military coups and one assassination. The island's recurrent political crises are often prolonged, with detrimental effects on the local economy, international relations and Malagasy living standards. The eight-month standoff between incumbent Ratsiraka and challenger Marc Ravalomanana following the 2001 presidential elections cost Madagascar millions of dollars in lost tourism and trade revenue as well as damage to infrastructure, such as bombed bridges and buildings damaged by arson.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/2009_Malagasy_protests" class="mw-redirect" title="2009 Malagasy protests">A series of protests</a> led by Andry Rajoelina against Ravalomanana in early 2009 became violent, with more than 170 people killed.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Modern politics in Madagascar are colored by the history of Merina subjugation of coastal communities under their rule in the 19th century. The consequent tension between the highland and coastal populations has periodically flared up into isolated events of violence.<sup id="cite_ref-ethnicstrife_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ethnicstrife-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madagascar has historically been perceived as being on the margin of mainstream African affairs despite being a founding member of the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity" title="Organisation of African Unity">Organisation of African Unity</a>, which was established in 1963 and dissolved in 2002 to be replaced by the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>. Madagascar was not permitted to attend the first African Union summit because of a dispute over the results of the 2001 presidential election, but rejoined the African Union in July 2003 after a 14-month hiatus. Madagascar was again suspended by the African Union in March 2009 following the <a href="/wiki/2009_Malagasy_political_crisis" title="2009 Malagasy political crisis">unconstitutional transfer of executive power</a> to Rajoelina.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar is a member of the <a href="/wiki/International_Criminal_Court" title="International Criminal Court">International Criminal Court</a> with a Bilateral Immunity Agreement of protection for the <a href="/wiki/United_States_military" class="mw-redirect" title="United States military">United States military</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Eleven countries have established embassies in Madagascar, including France, the United Kingdom, the United States, China and India,<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> while Madagascar has <a href="/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_Madagascar" title="List of diplomatic missions of Madagascar">embassies in sixteen other countries</a>.
</p><p>Human rights in Madagascar are protected under the constitution and the state is a signatory to numerous international agreements including the <a href="/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights" title="Universal Declaration of Human Rights">Universal Declaration of Human Rights</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Convention_on_the_Rights_of_the_Child" title="Convention on the Rights of the Child">Convention on the Rights of the Child</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DOS_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DOS-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Religious, ethnic and sexual minorities are protected under the law. Freedom of association and assembly are also guaranteed under the law, although in practice the denial of permits for public assembly has occasionally been used to impede political demonstrations.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-DOS_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DOS-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Torture by security forces is rare and state repression is low relative to other countries with comparably few legal safeguards, although arbitrary arrests and the corruption of military and police officers remain problems. Ravalomanana's 2004 creation of BIANCO, an anti-corruption bureau, resulted in reduced corruption among Antananarivo's lower-level bureaucrats in particular, although high-level officials have not been prosecuted by the bureau.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Accusations of media <a href="/wiki/Censorship" title="Censorship">censorship</a> have risen due to the alleged restrictions on the coverage of government opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Some journalists have been arrested for allegedly spreading <a href="/wiki/Fake_news" title="Fake news">fake news</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_and_law_enforcement">Military and law enforcement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Military and law enforcement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Military_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Military of Madagascar">Military of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Madagascar" title="Law enforcement in Madagascar">Law enforcement in Madagascar</a></div>
<p>The rise of centralized kingdoms among the Sakalava, Merina and other ethnic groups produced the island's first standing armies by the 16th century, initially equipped with spears but later with muskets, cannons and other firearms.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> By the early 19th century, the Merina sovereigns of the Kingdom of Madagascar had brought much of the island under their control by mobilizing an army of trained and armed soldiers numbering as high as 30,000.<sup id="cite_ref-army_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-army-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> French attacks on coastal towns in the later part of the century prompted then-Prime Minister Rainilaiarivony to solicit British assistance to provide training to the Merina monarchy's army. Despite the training and leadership provided by British military advisers, the Malagasy army was unable to withstand French weaponry and was forced to surrender following an attack on the royal palace at Antananarivo. Madagascar was declared a colony of France in 1897.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The political independence and sovereignty of the Malagasy armed forces, which comprises an army, navy and air force, was restored with independence from France in 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Since this time the Malagasy military has never engaged in armed conflict with another state or within its own borders, but has occasionally intervened to restore order during periods of political unrest. Under the socialist Second Republic, Admiral Didier Ratsiraka instated mandatory national armed or civil service for all young citizens regardless of sex, a policy that remained in effect from 1976 to 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> The armed forces are under the direction of the Minister of Defense and have remained largely neutral during times of political crisis, as during the protracted standoff between incumbent Ratsiraka and challenger Marc Ravalomanana in the disputed 2001 presidential elections, when the military refused to intervene in favor of either candidate. This tradition was broken in 2009, when a segment of the army defected to the side of Andry Rajoelina, then-mayor of Antananarivo, in support of his attempt to force President Ravalomanana from power.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Minister of Interior is responsible for the national police force, paramilitary force (<i>gendarmerie</i>) and the secret police.<sup id="cite_ref-justiceMada_149-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-justiceMada-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> The police and gendarmerie are stationed and administered at the local level. However, in 2009 fewer than a third of all communes had access to the services of these security forces, with most lacking local-level headquarters for either corps.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Traditional community tribunals, called <i>dina</i>, are presided over by elders and other respected figures and remain a key means by which justice is served in rural areas where state presence is weak. Historically, security has been relatively high across the island.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Violent crime rates are low, and criminal activities are predominantly crimes of opportunity such as pickpocketing and petty theft, although child prostitution, human trafficking and the production and sale of marijuana and other illegal drugs are increasing.<sup id="cite_ref-justiceMada_149-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-justiceMada-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Budget cuts since 2009 have severely impacted the national police force, producing a steep increase in criminal activity in recent years.<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Madagascar" title="Regions of Madagascar">Regions of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Districts_of_Madagascar" title="Districts of Madagascar">Districts of Madagascar</a></div>
<p>Madagascar is subdivided into 23 regions (<i>faritra</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The regions are further subdivided into 119 districts, 1,579 communes, and 17,485 <i>fokontany</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Regions_of_Madagascar.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Regions_of_Madagascar.svg/220px-Regions_of_Madagascar.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="399" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Regions_of_Madagascar.svg/330px-Regions_of_Madagascar.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Regions_of_Madagascar.svg/440px-Regions_of_Madagascar.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="803" data-file-height="1458" /></a><figcaption>A map of <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Madagascar" title="Regions of Madagascar">Madagascar's regions</a></figcaption></figure>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="United_Nations_involvement">United Nations involvement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: United Nations involvement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Madagascar became a member state of the United Nations on 20 September 1960, shortly after gaining its independence on 26 June 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> As of January 2017, 34 police officers from Madagascar are deployed in Haiti as part of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilisation_Mission_in_Haiti" title="United Nations Stabilisation Mission in Haiti">United Nations Stabilisation Mission in Haiti</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Starting in 2015, under the direction of and with assistance from the UN, the World Food Programme started the Madagascar Country Programme with the two main goals of long-term development and reconstruction efforts, and addressing the food insecurity issues in the southern regions of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> These goals plan to be accomplished by providing meals for specific schools in rural and urban priority areas and by developing national school feeding policies to increase consistency of nourishment throughout the country. Small and local farmers have also been assisted in increasing both the quantity and quality of their production, as well as improving their crop yield in unfavorable weather conditions.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> In 2017, Madagascar signed the UN <a href="/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons" title="Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons">treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Madagascar" title="Economy of Madagascar">Economy of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Madagascar" title="Tourism in Madagascar">Tourism in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg/220px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg/330px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg/440px-GDP_per_capita_development_of_Madagascar.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>Historical change in per capita GDP of Madagascar since 1950</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg/220px-Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg/330px-Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg/440px-Nosy-iranja-beach.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2120" data-file-height="1468" /></a><figcaption><a href="/w/index.php?title=Nosy_Iranja&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Nosy Iranja (page does not exist)">Nosy Iranja</a> is one of the international tourism destinations in Madagascar</figcaption></figure>
<p>Madagascar's GDP in 2015 was estimated at US$9.98&#160;billion, with a per capita GDP of $411.82.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Approximately 69&#160;percent of the population lives below the national poverty line threshold of one dollar per day.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> During 2011–15, the average growth rate was 2.6% but was expected to have reached 4.1% in 2016, due to public works programs and a growth of the service sector.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> The agriculture sector constituted 29&#160;percent of Malagasy GDP in 2011, while manufacturing formed 15&#160;percent of GDP. Madagascar's other sources of growth are tourism, agriculture and the extractive industries.<sup id="cite_ref-AEO2011_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AEO2011-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Tourism focuses on the niche eco-tourism market, capitalizing on Madagascar's unique biodiversity, unspoiled natural habitats, national parks and lemur species.<sup id="cite_ref-tourismsector_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tourismsector-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> An estimated 365,000 tourists visited Madagascar in 2008, but the sector declined during the political crisis with 180,000 tourists visiting in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-AEO2011_177-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AEO2011-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> However, the sector has been growing steadily for a few years; In 2016, 293,000 tourists landed in the African island with an increase of 20% compared to 2015; For 2017 the country has the goal of reaching 366,000 visitors, while for 2018 government estimates are expected to reach 500,000 annual tourists.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The island is still a very poor country in 2018; structural brakes remain in the development of the economy: corruption and the shackles of the public administration, lack of legal certainty, and backwardness of land legislation. The economy, however, has been growing since 2011, with GDP growth exceeding 4% per year;<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> almost all economic indicators are growing, the GDP per capita was around $1600 (PPP) for 2017,<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> one of the lowest in the world, although growing since 2012; unemployment was also cut, which in 2016 was equal to 2.1%<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> with a work force of 13.4&#160;million as of 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The main economic resources of Madagascar are <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_Madagascar" title="Tourism in Madagascar">tourism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Textile_industry" title="Textile industry">textiles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Agriculture_in_Madagascar" title="Agriculture in Madagascar">agriculture</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mining_industry_of_Madagascar" title="Mining industry of Madagascar">mining</a>.
</p><p>Poverty affects 92% of the population in 2017. The country ranks fourth in the world in terms of chronic malnutrition. Nearly one in two children under the age of five is stunted. In addition, Madagascar is among the five countries where access to water is the most difficult for the population. Twelve million people do not have access to clean water, according to the NGO WaterAid.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Natural_resources_and_trade">Natural resources and trade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Natural resources and trade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg/220px-Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg/330px-Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg/440px-Raffia_animals_created_by_artisans_in_Madagascar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Toy animals made from <a href="/wiki/Raffia" class="mw-redirect" title="Raffia">raffia</a>, a native palm<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Madagascar's natural resources include a variety of agricultural and mineral products. Agriculture (including the growing of <a href="/wiki/Raffia" class="mw-redirect" title="Raffia">raffia</a>), mining, fishing and <a href="/wiki/Forestry" title="Forestry">forestry</a> are mainstays of the economy. In 2017 the top exports were vanilla (US$894M), nickel metal (US$414M), cloves (US$288M), knitted sweaters (US$184M) and cobalt (US$143M).<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madagascar is the world's principal supplier of <a href="/wiki/Vanilla" title="Vanilla">vanilla</a>, cloves<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Ylang-ylang" class="mw-redirect" title="Ylang-ylang">ylang-ylang</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-gafilo_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gafilo-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The island supplies 80% of the world's natural vanilla.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Other key agricultural resources include coffee, <a href="/wiki/Lychee" title="Lychee">lychees</a> and shrimp. Key mineral resources include various types of precious and semi-precious stones, and it currently provides half of the world's supply of sapphires, which were discovered near <a href="/wiki/Ilakaka" title="Ilakaka">Ilakaka</a> in the late 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madagascar has one of the world's largest reserves of <a href="/wiki/Ilmenite" title="Ilmenite">ilmenite</a> (titanium ore), as well as important reserves of chromite, coal, iron, cobalt, copper and nickel.<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Several major projects are underway in the mining, <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">oil</a> and <a href="/wiki/Natural_gas" title="Natural gas">gas</a> sectors that are anticipated to give a significant boost to the Malagasy economy. These include such projects as ilmenite and zircon mining at the <a href="/wiki/Mandena" class="mw-redirect" title="Mandena">Mandena</a> mine by <a href="/wiki/Rio_Tinto_Group" class="mw-redirect" title="Rio Tinto Group">Rio Tinto</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-RioTinto_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RioTinto-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> extraction of nickel by the <a href="/wiki/Ambatovy_mine" title="Ambatovy mine">Ambatovy mine</a> near <a href="/wiki/Moramanga" title="Moramanga">Moramanga</a> and its processing near Toamasina by <a href="/wiki/Sherritt_International" title="Sherritt International">Sherritt International</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> and the development of the giant onshore <a href="/wiki/Heavy_crude_oil" title="Heavy crude oil">heavy oil</a> deposits at <a href="/wiki/Tsimiroro" title="Tsimiroro">Tsimiroro</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bemolanga" title="Bemolanga">Bemolanga</a> by <a href="/wiki/Madagascar_Oil" title="Madagascar Oil">Madagascar Oil</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Exports formed 28&#160;percent of GDP in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Most of the country's export revenue is derived from the textiles industry, fish and shellfish, vanilla, cloves and other foodstuffs.<sup id="cite_ref-AEO2011_177-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AEO2011-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> France is the nation's main trading partner, although the United States, Japan and Germany also have strong economic ties.<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar-U.S._Business_Council&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Madagascar-U.S. Business Council (page does not exist)">Madagascar-U.S. Business Council</a> was formed in May 2003, as a collaboration between <a href="/wiki/USAID" class="mw-redirect" title="USAID">USAID</a> and Malagasy artisan producers to support the export of local handicrafts to foreign markets.<sup id="cite_ref-handicrafts_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-handicrafts-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Imports of such items as foodstuffs, fuel, capital goods, vehicles, consumer goods and electronics consume an estimated 52&#160;percent of GDP. The main sources of Madagascar's imports include <a href="/wiki/China%E2%80%93Madagascar_relations" title="China–Madagascar relations">China</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> France, Iran, Mauritius and Hong Kong.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Infrastructure_and_media">Infrastructure and media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Infrastructure and media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Madagascar" title="Transport in Madagascar">Transport in Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Telecommunications in Madagascar">Telecommunications in Madagascar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Madagascar" title="List of newspapers in Madagascar">List of newspapers in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG/220px-Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG/330px-Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG/440px-Antananarivo_Madagascar_people_reading_news.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="3024" /></a><figcaption>A news stand in Antananarivo</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2010, Madagascar had approximately 7,617&#160;km (4,730&#160;mi) of paved roads, 854&#160;km (530&#160;mi) of railways and 432&#160;km (270&#160;mi) of navigable waterways.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradtp2_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradtp2-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The majority of <a href="/wiki/Roads_in_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Roads in Madagascar">roads in Madagascar</a> are unpaved, with many becoming impassable in the rainy season. Largely paved <a href="/wiki/National_routes_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="National routes of Madagascar">national routes</a> connect the six largest regional towns to Antananarivo, with minor paved and unpaved routes providing access to other population centers in each district.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Construction of the <a href="/wiki/Antananarivo%E2%80%93Toamasina_toll_highway" title="Antananarivo–Toamasina toll highway">Antananarivo–Toamasina toll highway</a>, the country's first <a href="/wiki/Toll_highway" class="mw-redirect" title="Toll highway">toll highway</a>, began in December 2022. The approximately <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">US$</a>1,000,000,000 infrastructure project, which will connect Madagascar's capital to its largest seaport, is expected to take four years to complete.<sup id="cite_ref-:12_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Another project meant to create 348 kilometers of roads and create better connections costs €235.5 million. This includes a €116 million grant from the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, a €110 million loan from the <a href="/wiki/European_Investment_Bank" title="European Investment Bank">European Investment Bank</a>, and €4.8 million in finance from the Republic of Madagascar.<b><sup id="cite_ref-:1403_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1403-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup></b> Since 2016, €100.4 million has been paid to the Republic of Madagascar through this project.<b><sup id="cite_ref-:1403_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1403-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup></b><sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are several <a href="/wiki/Rail_lines_in_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Rail lines in Madagascar">rail lines in Madagascar</a>. Antananarivo is connected to Toamasina, Ambatondrazaka and Antsirabe by rail, and another rail line connects Fianarantsoa to Manakara. The most important seaport in Madagascar is located on the east coast at Toamasina. Ports at Mahajanga and Antsiranana are significantly less used because of their remoteness.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar's government hopes to expand the ports of Antsiranana in the north and Taolagnaro in the south, connecting them to improved road networks, since many imports are every day necessities and Madagascar also relies on export money.<b><sup id="cite_ref-:1403_197-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1403-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup></b><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> The island's newest port at Ehoala, constructed in 2008 and privately managed by <a href="/wiki/Rio_Tinto_(corporation)" title="Rio Tinto (corporation)">Rio Tinto</a>, will come under state control upon completion of the company's mining project near Tôlanaro around 2038.<sup id="cite_ref-RioTinto_191-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RioTinto-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Air_Madagascar" title="Air Madagascar">Air Madagascar</a> services the island's many small regional airports, which offer the only practical means of access to many of the more remote regions during rainy season road washouts.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Running water and electricity are supplied at the national level by a government service provider, <a href="/wiki/Jirama" title="Jirama">Jirama</a>, which is unable to service the entire population. As of 2009<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, only 6.8&#160;percent of Madagascar's <i>fokontany</i> had access to water provided by Jirama, while 9.5&#160;percent had access to its electricity services.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Fifty-six percent of Madagascar's power is provided by hydroelectric power plants, with the remaining 44% provided by diesel engine generators.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Mobile telephone and internet access are widespread in urban areas but remain limited in rural parts of the island. Approximately 30% of the districts are able to access the nations' several private telecommunications networks via mobile telephones or land lines.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> estimates that 17 million people in Madagascar's rural areas live more than two kilometres away from an all-season road.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> In Madagascar, 11% of the rural population has access to power.<b><sup id="cite_ref-:1403_197-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1403-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup></b><sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Radio broadcasts remain the principal means by which the Malagasy population access international, national, and local news. Only state radio broadcasts are transmitted across the entire island. Hundreds of public and private stations with local or regional range provide alternatives to state broadcasting.<sup id="cite_ref-mediainfo_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mediainfo-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the state television channel, a variety of privately owned television stations broadcast local and international programming throughout Madagascar. Several media outlets are owned by political partisans or politicians themselves, including the media groups MBS (owned by Ravalomanana) and Viva (owned by Rajoelina),<sup id="cite_ref-CrossroadsMarcus_137-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CrossroadsMarcus-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> contributing to political polarization in reporting.
</p><p>The media have historically come under varying degrees of pressure to censor their criticism of the government. Reporters are occasionally threatened or harassed, and media outlets are periodically forced to close.<sup id="cite_ref-mediainfo_150-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mediainfo-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Accusations of media censorship have increased since 2009 because of the alleged intensification of restrictions on political criticism.<sup id="cite_ref-DOS_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DOS-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Access to the internet has grown dramatically over the past decade, with an estimated 352,000 residents of Madagascar accessing the internet from home or in one of the nation's many internet cafés in December 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-mediainfo_150-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mediainfo-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Madagascar" title="Demographics of Madagascar">Demographics of Madagascar</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Madagascar" title="List of cities in Madagascar">List of cities in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar_Map.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="map of Madagascar showing distribution of Malagasy ethnic subgroups" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar_Map.png/350px-Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar_Map.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="445" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/02/Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar_Map.png 1.5x" data-file-width="471" data-file-height="599" /></a><figcaption>The regional distribution of <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic groups of Madagascar">Malagasy ethnic subgroups</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Agriculture has long influenced settlement on the island. Only 15% of the nation's 24,894,551 population live in the 10 largest cities.
</p><p>In 2021, the population of Madagascar was estimated at 29&#160;million, up from 2.2&#160;million in 1900.<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The annual population growth rate in Madagascar was approximately 2.9&#160;percent in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Approximately 42.5&#160;percent of the population is younger than 15 years of age, while 54.5&#160;percent are between the ages of 15 and 64. Those aged 65 and older form 3 percent of the total population.<sup id="cite_ref-AEO2011_177-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AEO2011-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Only two general censuses, in 1975 and 1993, have been carried out after independence. The most densely populated regions of the island are the eastern highlands and the eastern coast, contrasting most dramatically with the sparsely populated western plains.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Ethnic groups">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic groups of Madagascar">Ethnic groups of Madagascar</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy people">Malagasy</a> ethnic group forms over 90&#160;percent of Madagascar's population and is typically divided into 18 ethnic subgroups.<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Recent DNA research revealed that the genetic makeup of the average Malagasy person constitutes an approximately equal blend of <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oceania" title="Oceania">Oceanian</a> and East African genes,<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Tofanelli_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tofanelli-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Cox_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cox-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> although the genetics of some communities show a predominance of Southeast Asian or East African origins or some Arab, <a href="/wiki/Indians_in_Madagascar" title="Indians in Madagascar">Indian</a>, or European ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-Adelaar_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Adelaar-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Southeast Asian features – specifically from the southern part of <a href="/wiki/Borneo" title="Borneo">Borneo</a> – are most predominant among the <a href="/wiki/Merina_people" title="Merina people">Merina</a> of the central highlands,<sup id="cite_ref-ethnicstrife_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ethnicstrife-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> who form the largest Malagasy ethnic subgroup at approximately 26&#160;percent of the population, while certain communities among the western coastal peoples (collectively called <i>côtiers</i>) have relatively stronger East African features. The largest coastal ethnic subgroups are the <a href="/wiki/Betsimisaraka_people" title="Betsimisaraka people">Betsimisaraka</a> (14.9&#160;percent) and the <a href="/wiki/Tsimihety" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsimihety">Tsimihety</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sakalava" class="mw-redirect" title="Sakalava">Sakalava</a> (6&#160;percent each).<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Peoples along the east and southeastern coasts often have a roughly equal blend of Austronesian and Bantu ancestry; coastal peoples also usually show the largest genetic influence from the centuries of Arab, Somali, Gujarati, and Tamil traders and merchants of the area, compared to the inland highlander peoples.
</p>
<table class="wikitable" style="margin: 1em auto 1em auto;">

<tbody><tr>

<td><a href="/wiki/Sakalava_people" title="Sakalava people">Sakalava</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vezo_people" title="Vezo people">Vezo</a></td>
<td>Former <a href="/wiki/Mahajanga_Province" title="Mahajanga Province">Mahajanga Province</a>; western coast
</td></tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="/wiki/Betsimisaraka_people" title="Betsimisaraka people">Betsimisaraka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sihanaka" title="Sihanaka">Sihanaka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bezanozano" title="Bezanozano">Bezanozano</a></td>
<td>Former <a href="/wiki/Toamasina_Province" title="Toamasina Province">Toamasina Province</a>; eastern coast
</td></tr>

<td>Former <a href="/wiki/Antananarivo_Province" title="Antananarivo Province">Antananarivo Province</a>; central highlands
</td></tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="/wiki/Betsileo_people" title="Betsileo people">Betsileo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antaifasy" title="Antaifasy">Antaifasy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antambahoaka" title="Antambahoaka">Antambahoaka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antemoro_people" title="Antemoro people">Antaimoro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antaisaka_people" title="Antaisaka people">Antaisaka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tanala" title="Tanala">Tanala</a></td>
<td>Former <a href="/wiki/Fianarantsoa_Province" title="Fianarantsoa Province">Fianarantsoa Province</a>; southeastern coast
</td></tr>
<tr>

<td>Former <a href="/wiki/Toliara_Province" title="Toliara Province">Toliara Province</a>; southern inland regions and coast
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><a href="/wiki/Chinese_people_in_Madagascar" title="Chinese people in Madagascar">Chinese</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indians_in_Madagascar" title="Indians in Madagascar">Indian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Comoros" title="Comoros">Comoran</a> minorities are present in Madagascar, as well as a small European (primarily <a href="/wiki/French_people" title="French people">French</a>) populace. Emigration in the late 20th century has reduced these minority populations, occasionally in abrupt waves, such as the exodus of Comorans in 1976, following anti-Comoran riots in Mahajanga.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> By comparison, there has been no significant emigration of Malagasy peoples.<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> The number of <a href="/wiki/French_people_in_Madagascar" title="French people in Madagascar">Europeans</a> has declined since independence, reduced from 68,430 in 1958<sup id="cite_ref-Kitchen_1962,_p._256_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kitchen_1962,_p._256-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> to 17,000 three decades later. There were an estimated 25,000&#160;Comorans, 18,000&#160;Indians, and 9,000&#160;Chinese living in Madagascar in the mid-1980s.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
