<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Madagascar_Kids_5_(4814978342).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg/220px-Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg/330px-Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg/440px-Madagascar_Kids_5_%284814978342%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1080" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>A Malagasy child</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy language</a> and <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Madagascar" title="Languages of Madagascar">Languages of Madagascar</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy language</a> is of <a href="/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian" class="mw-redirect" title="Malayo-Polynesian">Malayo-Polynesian</a> origin and is generally spoken throughout the island. The numerous dialects of Malagasy, which are generally mutually intelligible,<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> can be clustered under one of two subgroups: eastern Malagasy, spoken along the eastern forests and highlands including the Merina dialect of Antananarivo, and western Malagasy, spoken across the western coastal plains. The <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_language" title="Malagasy language">Malagasy language</a> derives from the Southeast <a href="/wiki/Barito_languages" title="Barito languages">Barito languages</a>, with the <a href="/wiki/Ma%27anyan_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ma&#39;anyan language">Ma'anyan language</a> being its closest relative, incorporating numerous <a href="/wiki/Malay_language" title="Malay language">Malay</a> and <a href="/wiki/Javanese_language" title="Javanese language">Javanese</a> loanwords.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> became the official language during the colonial period, when Madagascar came under the authority of France. In the first national Constitution of 1958, Malagasy and French were named the official languages of the Malagasy Republic. Madagascar is a <a href="/wiki/Francophone" class="mw-redirect" title="Francophone">francophone</a> country, and French is mostly spoken as a second language among the educated population and used for international communication.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>No official languages were mentioned in the Constitution of 1992, although Malagasy was identified as the national language. Nonetheless, many sources still claimed that Malagasy and French were official languages, eventually leading a citizen to initiate a legal case against the state in April 2000, on the grounds that the publication of official documents only in the French language was unconstitutional. The High Constitutional Court observed in its decision that, in the absence of a language law, French still had the character of an official language.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Constitution of 2007 recognised three official languages, Malagasy, French, and English.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> A fourth Constitution, adopted in 2010 following <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_constitutional_referendum,_2010" class="mw-redirect" title="Malagasy constitutional referendum, 2010">a referendum</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-4thRepublic_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4thRepublic-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> recognised only Malagasy and French.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Religion in Madagascar">Religion in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG/220px-Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="168" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG/330px-Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG/440px-Antsirabe_-_%C3%A9glise.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2550" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_La_Salette_Cathedral,_Antsirabe" title="Our Lady of La Salette Cathedral, Antsirabe">Our Lady of La Salette Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Antsirabe" title="Antsirabe">Antsirabe</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg/220px-130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg/330px-130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg/440px-130415-Eglise_de_Faravohitra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1397" data-file-height="926" /></a><figcaption>Faravohitra <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a> Church in <a href="/wiki/Antananarivo" title="Antananarivo">Antananarivo</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> is the most widely professed religion in Madagascar. According to the most recent national census completed in 1993, a majority of the population (52 percent) adhered to indigenous beliefs, with Christianity being the largest single religion at 41 percent, followed by <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> at 7 percent. However, according to the <a href="/wiki/Pew_Research_Center" title="Pew Research Center">Pew Research Center</a> in 2020, 85% of the population identified as <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Madagascar" title="Christianity in Madagascar">Christian</a>, while just 4.5% practiced folk religions; <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestants</a> comprise a plurality of Christians, followed by <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Madagascar" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholicism in Madagascar">Roman Catholics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-pew_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pew-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, a 2020 study conducted by the <a href="/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives" title="Association of Religion Data Archives">Association of Religion Data Archives</a> found 58.1% of the population was <a href="/wiki/Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian">Christian</a>, 2.1% <a href="/wiki/Muslim" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim">Muslim</a>, 39.2% practiced traditional faiths, and 0.6% was <a href="/wiki/Irreligious" class="mw-redirect" title="Irreligious">nonreligious</a> or adhered to other faiths.<sup id="cite_ref-National_Profiles_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Profiles-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The inconsistency in religious data reflects the common practice of alternating between religious identities or <a href="/wiki/Syncretism" title="Syncretism">syncretizing</a> different faith traditions. Christians integrate and combine their religious beliefs with the deeply rooted practice of honoring ancestors. For instance, they may bless their dead at church before proceeding with traditional burial rites or invite a <a href="/wiki/Minister_(Christianity)" title="Minister (Christianity)">Christian minister</a> to consecrate a <i>famadihana</i> reburial.<sup id="cite_ref-famadihana_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-famadihana-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Christianity is predominant in the highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> The Malagasy Council of Churches comprises the four oldest and most prominent Christian denominations of Madagascar (Roman Catholic, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Jesus_Christ_in_Madagascar" title="Church of Jesus Christ in Madagascar">Church of Jesus Christ in Madagascar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_Lutheran_Church" title="Malagasy Lutheran Church">Lutheran</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Province_of_the_Indian_Ocean" title="Church of the Province of the Indian Ocean">Anglican</a>) and has been influential in Malagasy politics.<sup id="cite_ref-USSD_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USSD-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Veneration_of_the_dead" title="Veneration of the dead">veneration of ancestors</a> has led to the widespread tradition of tomb building, as well as the highlands practice of the <i><a href="/wiki/Famadihana" title="Famadihana">famadihana</a></i>, whereby a deceased family member's remains are exhumed and re-wrapped in fresh silk shrouds, before being replaced in the tomb. The <i>famadihana</i> is an occasion to celebrate the beloved ancestor's memory, reunite with family and community, and enjoy a festive atmosphere. Residents of surrounding villages are often invited to attend the party, where food and rum are typically served, and a <i><a href="/wiki/Hiragasy" title="Hiragasy">hiragasy</a></i> troupe or other musical entertainment is commonly present.<sup id="cite_ref-famadihana_218-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-famadihana-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Consideration for ancestors is also demonstrated through adherence to <i><a href="/wiki/Fady_(taboo)" title="Fady (taboo)">fady</a></i>, taboos that are respected during and after the lifetime of the person who establishes them. It is widely believed that by showing respect for ancestors in these ways, they may intervene on behalf of the living. Conversely, misfortunes are often attributed to ancestors whose memory or wishes have been neglected. The sacrifice of <a href="/wiki/Zebu" title="Zebu">zebu</a> is a traditional method used to appease or honor the ancestors. In addition, the Malagasy traditionally believe in a creator god, called <a href="/wiki/Zanahary" title="Zanahary">Zanahary</a> or Andriamanitra.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradtbeliefs_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradtbeliefs-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Islam was first brought to Madagascar in the Middle Ages by <a href="/wiki/Arab_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab people">Arab</a> and <a href="/wiki/Somali_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Somali people">Somali</a> Muslim traders, who established several Islamic schools along the eastern coast. While the use of Arabic script and loan words, and the adoption of Islamic astrology, would spread across the island, Islam took hold in only a handful of southeastern coastal communities. In 2020, <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Madagascar" title="Islam in Madagascar">Muslims</a> constituted 2% of the population of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-National_Profiles_217-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Profiles-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> They are largely concentrated in the northwestern provinces of <a href="/wiki/Mahajanga" title="Mahajanga">Mahajanga</a> and <a href="/wiki/Antsiranana" title="Antsiranana">Antsiranana</a>. Muslims are divided between ethnic Malagasy and Indians, Pakistanis and Comorans.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hinduism</a> was introduced to Madagascar through <a href="/wiki/Gujarati_people" title="Gujarati people">Gujarati people</a> immigrating from the <a href="/wiki/Saurashtra_(region)" title="Saurashtra (region)">Saurashtra</a> region of <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a> in the late 19th century. Most Hindus in Madagascar speak <a href="/wiki/Gujarati_language" title="Gujarati language">Gujarati</a> or <a href="/wiki/Hindi" title="Hindi">Hindi</a> at home, reflecting the faiths concentration among those of Indian ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-id_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-id-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Healthcare_in_Madagascar" title="Healthcare in Madagascar">Healthcare in Madagascar</a></div>
<p>Medical centers, dispensaries, and hospitals are found throughout the island, although they are concentrated in urban areas and particularly in Antananarivo. Access to medical care remains beyond the reach of many Malagasy, especially in the rural areas, and many recourse to traditional healers.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the high expense of medical care relative to the average Malagasy income, the prevalence of trained medical professionals remains extremely low. In 2010, Madagascar had an average of three hospital beds per 10,000 people and a total of 3,150 doctors, 5,661 nurses, 385 community health workers, 175 pharmacists, and 57 dentists for a population of 22&#160;million. Fifteen percent of government spending in 2008 was directed toward the health sector. Approximately 70 percent of spending on health was contributed by the government, while 30 percent originated with international donors and other private sources.<sup id="cite_ref-WHO2011_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WHO2011-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> The government provides at least one basic health center per commune. Private health centers are concentrated within urban areas and particularly those of the central highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite these barriers to access, health services have shown a trend toward improvement over the past twenty years. Child immunizations against such diseases as <a href="/wiki/Hepatitis_B" title="Hepatitis B">hepatitis B</a>, <a href="/wiki/Diphtheria" title="Diphtheria">diphtheria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Measles" title="Measles">measles</a> increased an average of 60 percent in this period, indicating low but increasing availability of basic medical services and treatments. The Malagasy fertility rate in 2009 was 4.6 children per woman, declining from 6.3 in 1990. Teen pregnancy rates of 14.8 percent in 2011, much higher than the African average, are a contributing factor to rapid population growth.<sup id="cite_ref-WHO2011_224-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WHO2011-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> In 2010, the maternal mortality rate was 440 per 100,000 births, compared to 373.1 in 2008 and 484.4 in 1990, indicating a decline in perinatal care following the 2009 coup. The infant mortality rate in 2011 was 41 per 1,000 births,<sup id="cite_ref-BGNote_48-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BGNote-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> with an under-five mortality rate at 61 per 1,000 births.<sup id="cite_ref-SOWMY_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SOWMY-225">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Schistosomiasis" title="Schistosomiasis">Schistosomiasis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a>, and sexually transmitted diseases are common in Madagascar, although infection rates of <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS" title="HIV/AIDS">AIDS</a> remain low relative to many countries in mainland Africa, at 0.2 percent of the adult population. The malaria mortality rate is also among the lowest in Africa at 8.5 deaths per 100,000 people, in part because of the highest frequency use of insecticide treated nets in Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-WHO2011_224-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WHO2011-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Adult life expectancy in 2009 was 63 years for men and 67 years for women.<sup id="cite_ref-WHO2011_224-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WHO2011-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Madagascar had <a href="/wiki/21st_century_Madagascar_plague_outbreaks" title="21st century Madagascar plague outbreaks">outbreaks</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Bubonic_plague" title="Bubonic plague">bubonic plague</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pneumonic_plague" title="Pneumonic plague">pneumonic plague</a> in 2017 (2575 cases, 221 deaths) and 2014 (263 confirmed cases, 71 deaths).<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> In 2019, Madagascar had a <a href="/wiki/2018_Madagascar_measles_outbreak" title="2018 Madagascar measles outbreak">measles outbreak</a>, resulting in 118,000 cases and 1,688 deaths. In 2020, Madagascar was also affected by the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Madagascar" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Madagascar">COVID-19 pandemic</a>. Undernourishment and hunger rates were at 42% in 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> According to the United Nations, more than one million people in southern Madagascar are struggling to get enough to eat,<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> due to what could become the first <a href="/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_Madagascar_famine" title="2021–2022 Madagascar famine">famine</a> caused by climate change.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Madagascar" title="Education in Madagascar">Education in Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_(EPP)_Madagascar.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Students working in groups in classroom as teacher observes" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg/220px-Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg/330px-Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg/440px-Diego_Suarez_Antsiranana_urban_public_primary_school_%28EPP%29_Madagascar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2916" data-file-height="2386" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Education_in_Madagascar" title="Education in Madagascar">Education</a> access and quality were prioritized under <a href="/wiki/Marc_Ravalomanana" title="Marc Ravalomanana">Ravalomanana</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Prior to the 19th century, all education in Madagascar was informal and typically served to teach practical skills as well as social and cultural values, including respect for ancestors and elders.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The first formal European-style school was established in 1818 at <a href="/wiki/Toamasina" title="Toamasina">Toamasina</a> by members of the <a href="/wiki/London_Missionary_Society" title="London Missionary Society">London Missionary Society</a> (LMS). The LMS was invited by King Radama I to expand its schools throughout Imerina to teach basic literacy and numeracy to aristocratic children. The schools were closed by Ranavalona I in 1835,<sup id="cite_ref-Ralibera_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ralibera-230">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> but reopened and expanded in the decades after her death.
</p><p>By the end of the 19th century, Madagascar had the most developed and modern school system in pre-colonial Sub-Saharan Africa. Access to schooling was expanded in coastal areas during the colonial period, with French language and basic work skills becoming the focus of the curriculum. During the post-colonial First Republic, a continued reliance on French nationals as teachers, and French as the language of instruction, displeased those desiring a complete separation from the former colonial power.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, under the socialist Second Republic, French instructors and other nationals were expelled, Malagasy was declared the language of instruction, and a large cadre of young Malagasy were rapidly trained to teach at remote rural schools under the mandatory two-year national service policy.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This policy, known as <i>malgachization</i>, coincided with a severe economic downturn and a dramatic decline in the quality of education. Those schooled during this period generally failed to master the French language or many other subjects and struggled to find employment, forcing many to take low-paying jobs in the informal or black market that mired them in deepening poverty. Excepting the brief presidency of Albert Zafy, from 1992 to 1996, Ratsiraka remained in power from 1975 to 2001 and failed to achieve significant improvements in education throughout his tenure.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Education was prioritized under the Ravalomanana administration (2002–09), and is currently free and compulsory from ages 6 to 13.<sup id="cite_ref-WBeducation_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WBeducation-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> The primary schooling cycle is five years, followed by four years at the lower secondary level and three years at the upper secondary level.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> During Ravalomanana's first term, thousands of new primary schools and additional classrooms were constructed, older buildings were renovated, and tens of thousands of new primary teachers were recruited and trained. Primary school fees were eliminated, and kits containing basic school supplies were distributed to primary students.<sup id="cite_ref-WBeducation_233-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WBeducation-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Government school construction initiatives have ensured at least one primary school per <i>fokontany</i> and one lower secondary school within each commune. At least one upper secondary school is located in each of the larger urban centers.<sup id="cite_ref-INSTAT_166-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INSTAT-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> The three branches of the national public university are located at Antananarivo, Mahajanga, and Fianarantsoa. These are complemented by public teacher-training colleges and several private universities and technical colleges.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a result of increased educational access, enrollment rates more than doubled between 1996 and 2006. However, education quality is weak, producing high rates of grade repetition and dropout.<sup id="cite_ref-WBeducation_233-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WBeducation-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> Education policy in Ravalomanana's second term focused on quality issues, including an increase in minimum education standards for the recruitment of primary teachers from a middle school leaving certificate (BEPC) to a high school leaving certificate (BAC), and a reformed teacher training program to support the transition from traditional didactic instruction to student-centered teaching methods to boost student learning and participation in the classroom.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Public expenditure on education was 2.8&#160;percent of GDP in 2014. The literacy rate is estimated at 64.7%.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Madagascar" title="Culture of Madagascar">Culture of Madagascar</a></div>
<p>Each of the many ethnic subgroups in Madagascar adhere to their own set of beliefs, practices and ways of life that have historically contributed to their unique identities. However, there are a number of core cultural features that are common throughout the island, creating a strongly unified Malagasy cultural identity. In addition to a common language and shared traditional religious beliefs around a creator god and veneration of the ancestors, the traditional Malagasy worldview is shaped by values that emphasize <i>fihavanana</i> (solidarity), <i>vintana</i> (destiny), <i>tody</i> (karma), and <i><a href="/wiki/Hasina_(Madagascar)" title="Hasina (Madagascar)">hasina</a></i>, a sacred life force that traditional communities believe imbues and thereby legitimates authority figures within the community or family. Other cultural elements commonly found throughout the island include the practice of male circumcision; strong kinship ties; a widespread belief in the power of magic, diviners, astrology and witch doctors; and a traditional division of social classes into nobles, commoners, and slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-LOC_47-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LOC-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradtbeliefs_221-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradtbeliefs-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although social castes are no longer legally recognized, ancestral caste affiliation often continues to affect social status, economic opportunity, and roles within the community.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> Malagasy people traditionally consult <i>Mpanandro</i> ("Makers of the Days") to identify the most auspicious days for important events such as weddings or <i>famadihana</i>, according to a traditional astrological system introduced by Arabs. Similarly, the nobles of many Malagasy communities in the pre-colonial period would commonly employ advisers known as the <i>ombiasy</i> (from <i>olona-be-hasina</i>, "man of much virtue") of the southeastern <a href="/wiki/Antemoro_people" title="Antemoro people">Antemoro</a> ethnic group, who trace their ancestry back to early Somali settlers.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The diverse origins of Malagasy culture are evident in its tangible expressions. The most emblematic instrument of Madagascar, the <i><a href="/wiki/Valiha" title="Valiha">valiha</a></i>, is a <a href="/wiki/Bamboo" title="Bamboo">bamboo</a> <a href="/wiki/Tube_zither" title="Tube zither">tube zither</a> carried to Madagascar by early settlers from <a href="/wiki/South_Kalimantan" title="South Kalimantan">southern Borneo</a>, and is very similar in form to those found in <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Indonesia" title="Music of Indonesia">Indonesia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Music_of_the_Philippines" title="Music of the Philippines">Philippines</a> today.<sup id="cite_ref-Blench,_R._1982_pp._81-93_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blench,_R._1982_pp._81-93-237">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Traditional <a href="/wiki/Architecture_of_Madagascar" title="Architecture of Madagascar">houses in Madagascar</a> are likewise similar to those of southern Borneo in terms of symbolism and construction, featuring a rectangular layout with a peaked roof and central support pillar.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> Reflecting a widespread veneration of the ancestors, tombs are culturally significant in many regions and tend to be built of more durable material, typically stone, and display more elaborate decoration than the houses of the living.<sup id="cite_ref-Acquier,_Jean-Louis._239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Acquier,_Jean-Louis.-239">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> The production and weaving of silk can be traced back to the island's earliest settlers, and Madagascar's national dress, the woven <i><a href="/wiki/Lamba_(garment)" title="Lamba (garment)">lamba</a></i>, has evolved into a varied and refined art.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Southeast Asian cultural influence is also evident in <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_cuisine" title="Malagasy cuisine">Malagasy cuisine</a>, in which rice is consumed at every meal, typically accompanied by one of a variety of flavorful vegetable or meat dishes.<sup id="cite_ref-Antal_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Antal-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> African influence is reflected in the sacred importance of zebu cattle and their embodiment of their owner's wealth, traditions originating on the African mainland. <a href="/wiki/Cattle_raiding" title="Cattle raiding">Cattle rustling</a>, originally a rite of passage for young men in the plains areas of Madagascar where the largest herds of cattle are kept, has become a dangerous and sometimes deadly criminal enterprise as herdsmen in the southwest attempt to defend their cattle with traditional spears against increasingly armed professional rustlers.<sup id="cite_ref-Camp93_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Camp93-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arts">Arts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Arts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Madagascar" title="Music of Madagascar">Music of Madagascar</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg/170px-Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="278" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg/255px-Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg/340px-Hira_gasy_dancer_Madagascar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="3712" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Hiragasy" title="Hiragasy">Hiragasy</a> dancer.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A wide variety of oral and written <a href="/wiki/Literature_of_Madagascar" title="Literature of Madagascar">literature</a> has developed in Madagascar. One of the island's foremost artistic traditions is its oratory, as expressed in the forms of <i><a href="/wiki/Hainteny" title="Hainteny">hainteny</a></i> (poetry), <i>kabary</i> (public discourse) and <i>ohabolana</i> (proverbs).<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Madatana_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Madatana-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> An epic poem exemplifying these traditions, the <i><a href="/wiki/Ibonia" title="Ibonia">Ibonia</a></i>, has been handed down over the centuries in several different forms across the island, and offers insight into the diverse mythologies and beliefs of traditional Malagasy communities.<sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> This tradition was continued in the 20th century by such artists as <a href="/wiki/Jean-Joseph_Rabearivelo" title="Jean-Joseph Rabearivelo">Jean-Joseph Rabearivelo</a>, who is considered Africa's first modern poet,<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Elie_Rajaonarison" title="Elie Rajaonarison">Elie Rajaonarison</a>, an exemplar of the new wave of Malagasy poetry.<sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar has also developed a rich <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Madagascar" title="Music of Madagascar">musical heritage</a>, embodied in dozens of regional musical genres such as the coastal <i><a href="/wiki/Salegy" title="Salegy">salegy</a></i> or highland <i><a href="/wiki/Hiragasy" title="Hiragasy">hiragasy</a></i> that enliven village gatherings, local dance floors and national airwaves.<sup id="cite_ref-Randrianary_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Randrianary-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> Madagascar also has a growing culture of classical music fostered through youth academies, organizations and orchestras that promote youth involvement in classical music.
</p><p>The plastic arts are also widespread throughout the island. In addition to the tradition of silk weaving and lamba production, the weaving of <a href="/wiki/Raffia_palm" title="Raffia palm">raffia</a> and other local plant materials has been used to create a wide array of practical items such as floor mats, baskets, purses and hats.<sup id="cite_ref-handicrafts_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-handicrafts-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Wood carving is a highly developed art form, with distinct regional styles evident in the decoration of balcony railings and other architectural elements. Sculptors create a variety of furniture and household goods, <i>aloalo</i> funerary posts, and wooden sculptures, many of which are produced for the tourist market.<sup id="cite_ref-Terrible_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Terrible-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> The decorative and functional woodworking traditions of the <a href="/wiki/Zafimaniry" title="Zafimaniry">Zafimaniry</a> people of the central highlands was inscribed on UNESCO's <a href="/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists" title="UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage Lists">list of Intangible Cultural Heritage</a> in 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among the Antaimoro people, the production of paper embedded with flowers and other decorative natural materials is a long-established tradition that the community has begun to market to eco-tourists.<sup id="cite_ref-Terrible_248-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Terrible-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> Embroidery and <a href="/wiki/Drawn_thread_work" title="Drawn thread work">drawn thread work</a> are done by hand to produce clothing, as well as tablecloths and other home textiles for sale in local crafts markets.<sup id="cite_ref-handicrafts_194-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-handicrafts-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Malagasy artists such as <a href="/wiki/Madame_Zo" title="Madame Zo">Madame Zo</a> have incorporated textile traditions of Madagascar directly into their work.<sup id="cite_ref-Artnet_News_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Artnet_News-250">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> A small but growing number of fine art galleries in Antananarivo, and several other urban areas, offer paintings by local artists, and annual art events, such as the Hosotra open-air exhibition in the capital, contribute to the continuing development of fine arts in Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Sport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg/220px-Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg/330px-Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg/440px-Moraingy_fighting_Madagascar_sport.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1617" data-file-height="1301" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Moraingy" title="Moraingy">Moraingy</a></i> is a traditional martial art of Madagascar.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A number of traditional pastimes have emerged in Madagascar. <i><a href="/wiki/Moraingy" title="Moraingy">Moraingy</a></i>, a type of hand-to-hand combat, is a popular spectator sport in coastal regions. It is traditionally practiced by men, but women have recently begun to participate.<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> The wrestling of <a href="/wiki/Zebu" title="Zebu">zebu</a> cattle, which is named savika or <i>tolon-omby</i>, is also practiced in many regions.<sup id="cite_ref-lambatradition_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lambatradition-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> In addition to sports, a wide variety of games are played. Among the most emblematic is <i><a href="/wiki/Fanorona" title="Fanorona">fanorona</a></i>, a board game widespread throughout the Highland regions. According to folk legend, the succession of King <a href="/wiki/Andrianjaka" title="Andrianjaka">Andrianjaka</a> after his father <a href="/wiki/Ralambo" title="Ralambo">Ralambo</a> was partially the result of the obsession that Andrianjaka's older brother may have had with playing <i>fanorona</i> to the detriment of his other responsibilities.<sup id="cite_ref-iarivo_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iarivo-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Western recreational activities were introduced to Madagascar over the past two centuries. <a href="/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Madagascar" title="Rugby union in Madagascar">Rugby union</a> is considered the national sport of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Football_in_Madagascar" title="Football in Madagascar">Soccer</a> is also popular. Madagascar has produced a world champion in <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A9tanque" title="Pétanque">pétanque</a>, a French game similar to <a href="/wiki/Lawn_bowling" class="mw-redirect" title="Lawn bowling">lawn bowling</a>, which is widely played in urban areas and throughout the Highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> School athletics programs typically include soccer, track and field, judo, boxing, women's basketball and women's tennis. Madagascar sent its first competitors to the <a href="/wiki/1964_Summer_Olympics" title="1964 Summer Olympics">Olympic Games</a> in 1964, and has also competed in the <a href="/wiki/African_Games" title="African Games">African Games</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EBLand_98-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBLand-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Scouting" title="Scouting">Scouting</a> is represented in Madagascar by its <a href="/wiki/Firaisan%27ny_Skotisma_eto_Madagasikara" title="Firaisan&#39;ny Skotisma eto Madagasikara">own local federation</a> of three scouting clubs. Membership in 2011 was estimated at 14,905.<sup id="cite_ref-Census_2010_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census_2010-257">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because of its advanced sports facilities, Antananarivo gained the hosting rights for several of Africa's top international basketball events, including the <a href="/wiki/2011_FIBA_Africa_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="2011 FIBA Africa Championship">2011 FIBA Africa Championship</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/2009_FIBA_Africa_Championship_for_Women" title="2009 FIBA Africa Championship for Women">2009 FIBA Africa Championship for Women</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/2014_FIBA_Africa_Under-18_Championship" title="2014 FIBA Africa Under-18 Championship">2014 FIBA Africa Under-18 Championship</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/2013_FIBA_Africa_Under-16_Championship" title="2013 FIBA Africa Under-16 Championship">2013 FIBA Africa Under-16 Championship</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/2015_FIBA_Africa_Under-16_Championship_for_Women" title="2015 FIBA Africa Under-16 Championship for Women">2015 FIBA Africa Under-16 Championship for Women</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Madagascar%27s_national_3x3_basketball_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Madagascar&#39;s national 3x3 basketball team">Madagascar's national 3x3 basketball team</a> won the gold medal at the <a href="/wiki/3x3_basketball_at_the_2019_African_Games" title="3x3 basketball at the 2019 African Games">2019 African Games</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Cuisine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Malagasy_cuisine" title="Malagasy cuisine">Malagasy cuisine</a></div>
<p>Malagasy cuisine reflects the diverse influences of <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_cuisines" title="List of Asian cuisines">Southeast Asian</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_cuisine" title="African cuisine">African</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oceanic_cuisine" title="Oceanic cuisine">Oceania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_cuisine" title="Indian cuisine">Indian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chinese_cuisine" title="Chinese cuisine">Chinese</a>, and <a href="/wiki/European_cuisine" title="European cuisine">European</a> culinary traditions. The complexity of Malagasy meals can range from the simple, traditional preparations introduced by the earliest settlers, to the refined festival dishes prepared for the island's 19th-century monarchs. Throughout almost the entire island, the contemporary cuisine of Madagascar typically consists of a base of rice (<i>vary</i>) served with an accompaniment (<i>laoka</i>). The many varieties of <i>laoka</i> may be <a href="/wiki/Vegetarian_cuisine" title="Vegetarian cuisine">vegetarian</a> or include animal proteins, and typically feature a sauce flavored with such ingredients as ginger, onion, garlic, tomato, vanilla, coconut milk, salt, curry powder, green peppercorns or, less commonly, other spices or herbs. In parts of the arid south and west, pastoral families may replace rice with maize, <a href="/wiki/Cassava" title="Cassava">cassava</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Curd" title="Curd">curds</a> made from fermented <a href="/wiki/Zebu" title="Zebu">zebu</a> milk. A wide variety of sweet and savory <a href="/wiki/Fritter" title="Fritter">fritters</a> as well as other street foods are available across the island, as are diverse tropical and temperate-climate fruits. Locally produced beverages include fruit juices, coffee, herbal teas and teas, and alcoholic drinks such as <a href="/wiki/Rum" title="Rum">rum</a>, wine, and beer.<sup id="cite_ref-Antal_241-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Antal-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Three_Horses_Beer" title="Three Horses Beer">Three Horses Beer</a> is the most popular beer on the island<sup id="cite_ref-THBpilsner_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-THBpilsner-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> and is considered emblematic of Madagascar.<sup id="cite_ref-promos_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-promos-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Madagascar&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
